Unified error bounds for perturbations of non‑Markovian open quantum systems in Gaussian environments
Zhen Huang, Yuanran Zhu, Gunhee Park, Lin Lin

Abstract
We present perturbative error bounds for the non-Markovian dynamics of observables in open quantum systems interacting with Gaussian environments, governed by a general Liouville dynamics. This extends the work of [Mascherpa et al., Phys. Rev. Lett. 118, 100401, 2017], which demonstrated qualitatively tighter bounds over the standard Grönwall-type inequality for unitary system-bath evolution. Our results apply to systems with both bosonic and fermionic environments. Our approach utilizes a superoperator formalism, which avoids the need for formal coherent state path integral calculations, or the dilation of Lindblad dynamics into an equivalent unitary framework with infinitely many degrees of freedom. This enables a unified treatment of a wide range of open quantum systems. These findings provide a solid theoretical basis for various recently developed pseudomode methods in simulating open quantum system dynamics.



1
Introduction

Quantum systems that interact with environments characterized by a large number of degrees of freedom are known as open quantum systems
[
BP07
]
. Under certain conditions, such as weak coupling or the presence of well-separated time scales, a Markovian approximation may be effectively applied, allowing for the neglect of environmental memory effects. However, in general cases where information exchange occurs between the system and environment, these non-Markovian memory effects become significant, rendering the Markovian approximation insufficient. Such non-Markovian effects play a critical role in various disciplines, including condensed matter physics
[
LCD+87
,
Hew93
]
, solid-state quantum devices
[
CB08
,
RB15
]
, chemical physics
[
IF09
,
HP13
]
, quantum thermodynamics
[
EOG15
,
TH20
]
and quantum computation
[
LW23
]
.

Consider the dynamics of a quantum system (denoted by S) coupled with an environment (denoted by E), with
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
being the Hilbert spaces for the system and the environment, respectively. The total Hilbert space is
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
. Given an observable
\hat{O}_{S}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
, the goal is to compute its time-dependent expectation value denoted by
O_{\text{S}}(t)
by tracing out the contribution from
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
(see
Eq.

2.8
,
Eq.

2.9
). The most common example is that the system and environment together are described by a unitary dynamics on
\mathcal{H}
. By tracing out
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
, the effective dynamics (called an open quantum dynamics) on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
is in general non-Markovian. To make this problem analytically tractable, the environment as well as the system-environment coupling must be sufficiently simple. For instance, in a spin-boson model, the spin degree of freedom is viewed as the system, and the bosonic modes are viewed as the environment. In a fermionic impurity model, a few fermionic modes describing the fermionic impurities are viewed as the system, and the remaining fermionic modes describing the conduction electrons are viewed as the environment. Many numerical methods for simulating open quantum systems
[
PCHP10
,
dVSW15
,
DVA17
,
TSL+19
]
are formulated in this unitary setting.

However, when
\mathcal{H}
is a finite-dimensional space, the unitary dynamics on
\mathcal{H}
only has a finite number of modes. Therefore, a large number of degrees of freedom in the environment may be needed to describe the long-time behavior of the system dynamics correctly. An alternative way to tackle this problem is to consider a more general, non-unitary Liouville dynamics on
\mathcal{H}
, such as the Lindblad dynamics. This Liouville description encompasses the unitary dynamics as a special case. It is also compatible with the unitary description, in the sense that a Lindblad dynamics can always be described by a unitary dynamics using an infinitely sized environment via a dilation process
[
HP84
,
TSHP18
]
. The advantage is that the number of degrees needed for the environment using the Lindblad dynamics can be significantly smaller. This is the basis of various “pseudomode” theories used in practice
[
Gar97
,
SGD+16
,
TSHP18
,
MSS+20
,
LWvDG20
,
BMAP+20
,
WERZ20
,
TMC21
,
LGVF24
]
.

In this paper, we consider the open quantum system dynamics undergoing a general Liouville dynamics (see
Section

2.1
for the setup in an abstract setting, and
Section

3
for applications to spin-boson and fermionic models). We assume that in the interaction picture formulation, each system-environment coupling operator satisfies Wick’s conditions (see
Definition

2.6
). Such an environment is called Gaussian, since the multi-point correlation function is characterized by the two-point bath correlation functions (BCFs), denoted as
C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})
(
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1,\cdots,N
)
1
1

1

This is the most common scenario in practice. Our results can also be stated with respect to a more general bath correlation function of the form
C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})
(see
Eq.

2.39
).
. Here,
N
denotes the number of system-environment coupling operators. In such systems, the environment’s influence on the reduced system dynamics is completely described by the BCF
[
BP07
]
. However, we may only have approximate knowledge of the BCF, e.g., its values may be obtained by fitting to experimental results. Our main question is:

How do errors in the bath correlation functions affect the expectation values of system observables?

Specifically, for a given system observable
\hat{O}_{S}
, if the two-point BCF is perturbed by a small quantity
\Delta C(t-t^{\prime})
, how does the time-dependent expectation value
O_{\text{S}}(t)
depend on the perturbation?

Since the Liouville dynamics is linear, we can derive the following error estimate for
O_{\text{S}}(t)
, which we refer to as a Grönwall-type bound due to its resemblance to bounds obtained using the classical Grönwall inequality:

Theorem 1.1

(Grönwall-type error bound for system observables)
.

Let
C(t-t^{\prime})
and
C^{\prime}(t-t^{\prime})=C(t-t^{\prime})+\Delta C(t-t^{\prime})
be two-point BCFs corresponding to two different environments. For a bounded system operator
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
, let
O_{\text{S}}(t)
,
O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
be the expectation value of system observables corresponding to the two environments. Then, we have

|O_{\text{S}}(t)-O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)|\leq\|\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\|{\epsilon_{1}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{M}_{1}t},\quad\text{ for }t\in[0,T],

where
\|\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\|
is the operator norm of
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\max\{\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])},\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|C^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}\}
,
\epsilon_{1}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}-C^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}
, and
\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}[0,T]}
is the
L^{1}
norm of a function on
[0,T]
.

According to
Theorem

1.1
, achieving a precision of
\epsilon
for
O_{\text{S}}(t)
up to time
T
requires that the BCF be computed with a precision of
\epsilon_{1}=O\left(\epsilon e^{-\mathcal{M}_{1}T}\right)
. This stringent requirement becomes impractical for any time
T
beyond
\log(1/\epsilon)
. In this work, we establish a substantially improved error bound that relaxes the required precision to
\epsilon_{1}=O\left(\epsilon/T\right)
. Such an improvement allows simulations to be conducted over significantly longer time intervals while maintaining controlled precision on system observables.

Theorem 1.2

(Main result, for system observables, also see
Theorem

2.9
)
.

Let
C(t-t^{\prime})
,
C^{\prime}(t-t^{\prime})
,
O_{\text{S}}(t)
,
O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
and
\epsilon_{1}
be the same as in Lemma
1.1
. Then, we have

|O_{\text{S}}(t)-O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)|\leq\|\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\|(\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon_{1}t}-1),\quad\text{ for }t\in[0,T].

This error bound can be directly applied to simulating non-Markovian open quantum systems using pseudomode theories based on the Lindblad dynamics
[
Gar97
,
SGD+16
,
TSHP18
,
MSS+20
,
LWvDG20
,
BMAP+20
,
WERZ20
,
TMC21
,
LGVF24
]
. To further reduce the number of degrees of freedom in the environment, a number of “quasi-Lindblad” pseudomode theories have also been proposed in recent years
[
LACN19
,
PGP20
,
CLL+23
,
MFC+24
,
PHZ+24
,
TVA24
]
, which make use of non-Hermitian dynamics, or dynamics that may violate the complete positivity (CP) condition. We establish an error bound analogous to
Theorem

1.2
in
Corollary

4.1
under the setting of
[
PHZ+24
]
. Our result is also relevant beyond pseudomode theories, such as in the context of hierarchical equations of motion
[
TK89
,
Tan20
,
XYS+22
,
DXS+23
,
LRC+23
,
XVK+23
,
ILL24
]
, which can be interpreted as a certain quasi-Lindblad dynamics
[
XVK+23
,
ILL24
]
.

To our knowledge, an improved error bound similar to
Theorem

1.2
was first proved in
[
MSHP17
]
for spin-boson systems undergoing a unitary dynamics. Their proof uses coherent state path integrals. However, the spin coherent path integrals, and other continuous-time coherent state path integrals, may exhibit subtleties and unexpected breakdowns
[
WG11
,
Koc19
]
. As a result, derivations based on coherent state path integrals should not be identified with rigorous proofs in general (see also
[
KMK14
,
KKK16
]
). Recently,
[
LL24
]
provides a rigorous proof under the settings of
[
MSHP17
]
for a unitary spin-boson dynamics, using a diagrammatic and combinatorial argument similar to that used in the analysis of inchworm algorithms
[
CLY20
]
. It is not yet clear whether the framework in
[
LL24
]
could be easily generalized to non-unitary dynamics without much complication, especially with Lindblad-like jump operators. Furthermore, though all such arguments could be generalized to fermionic systems in principle, the fermionic case involves the Grassmann number (see, e.g.,
[
NO98
]
) or equivalent algebraic structures, which can be technically more complicated than their bosonic counterpart. To our knowledge, an error bound of the type of
Theorem

1.2
has not been established in the literature for fermionic systems.

In this paper, we provide a mathematically rigorous proof of the error bound in
Theorem

1.2
using the superoperator formalism
[
AKG20
,
CKC+22
]
. This method is simple and rigorous and provides a unified analysis framework for various physical settings. By
unified
, we mean that it treats unitary and non-unitary dynamics, as well as bosonic and fermionic environments, on the same footing. In particular, in the case of the fermionic environment, this approach avoids direct manipulation of Grassmann algebras.

This article is organized as follows. In
Section

2.1
, we first describe our problem setup, introduce the general Liouville dynamics for open quantum systems, and introduce Wick’s conditions. In
Section

2.3
, we give a proof for the Grönwall-type error bound (see
Theorem

2.8
).
Section

2.4
presents the main results of the paper, where we also provide an informal yet intuitive argument why this improved error bound holds. We give a rigorous proof for our improved error bound (see
Theorem

2.9
) in
Section

2.5
, which is valid for general Liouville dynamics. The applications of the main theorem to different open quantum system dynamics are considered in
Section

3
, where we use the spin-boson model (
Section

3.1
) and fermionic impurity model (
Section

3.2
) as examples for bosonic and fermionic environments, respectively. For both models, we discuss the original unitary dynamics and Lindblad pseudomode dynamics, demonstrating our results for both unitary and non-unitary cases. Finally, we discuss the quasi-Lindblad dynamics in
Section

4
, which violates the CP condition and contractive property.

Data Availability

No new data were created or analyzed in this study. Data sharing is not applicable to this article.

2
Error bounds of open quantum systems under Liouville dynamics

We consider the time evolution of open quantum systems under a Liouville dynamics. In this section, after introducing the problem setup, we obtain the Dyson series for a reduced system density operator (see
Eq.

2.25
). A Grönwall-type error bound can be derived directly from the series expansion of this Dyson series. Achieving a sharper bound, however, requires a resummation of the infinite series. Formally, this improved error bound can be obtained via (anti-)commuting operators under time-ordering. This formal proof is made rigorous by employing a rigorous combinatorial argument.

This section provides a unified framework for various physical settings of interest to this work, while in
Section

3
and
Section

4
, we introduce concrete examples for both bosonic and fermionic environments, and for both unitary and non-unitary dynamics, demonstrating the applications of
Eq.

2.25
and
Theorem

2.9
in different scenarios.

2.1
Preliminaries

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
denote the Hilbert spaces of the (sub)system and the environment, respectively. The total Hilbert space of the combined system is then given by
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
. We assume the subsystem is finite-dimensional. The environment
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
is assumed to be a separable Hilbert space, potentially infinite-dimensional (e.g., a bosonic or fermionic Fock space). We denote by
B(\mathcal{H})
the Banach space of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
, equipped with the operator norm
\|\cdot\|
. We denote by
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
the Banach space of trace-class operators on
\mathcal{H}
, equipped with the trace norm
\|\cdot\|_{\text{tr}}
. The state of the system is described by a density operator
\hat{\rho}\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, which is positive semi-definite and has unit trace.

The time evolution of the density operator
\hat{\rho}(t)
for the composite system (S + E) is governed by a strongly continuous one-parameter semigroup
(\Phi_{t})_{t\geq 0}=(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, such that

\hat{\rho}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t}\hat{\rho}(0).

(2.1)

The generator
\bm{L}
, or the Liouvillian, is a densely defined, closed superoperator (linear map) on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. We assume the Liouvillian
\bm{L}
decomposes according to the system-environment structure:

\bm{L}=\bm{L}_{\text{0}}+\bm{L}_{\text{\text{SE}}}.

(2.2)

To define these components, we utilize the tensor product of superoperators. Let
\bm{S}
and
\bm{E}
act on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
and
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}})
, respectively. The tensor product
\bm{E}\otimes\bm{S}
is defined on elementary tensors by

(\bm{E}\otimes\bm{S})(\hat{A}\otimes\hat{B}):=\bm{E}(\hat{A})\otimes\bm{S}(\hat{B}),

(2.3)

and extended by linearity and continuity (or by taking the closure, if the operators are unbounded).

Let
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
and
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
be the superoperators acting on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
and
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}})
, respectively. The free part of the Liouvillian is

\bm{L}_{0}=(\bm{1}_{\text{e}}\otimes\bm{L}_{\text{s}})+(\bm{L}_{\text{e}}\otimes\bm{1}_{\text{s}}),

(2.4)

where
\bm{1}_{\text{s}}
and
\bm{1}_{\text{e}}
are the respective identity superoperators. The interaction Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}
takes a separable form:

\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\bm{E}_{\alpha}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha}.

(2.5)

Since
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
is assumed to be finite dimensional,
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
is bounded. In contrast, when
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
is infinite-dimensional, the Liouvillian
\bm{L}
is typically unbounded. We assume that
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}
satisfies conditions relative to
\bm{L}_{0}
(e.g., relative boundedness) such that the closure of their sum is the generator of a strongly continuous semigroup on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})

[
EN00
,
CF98
]
.

An important case is when the composite system is closed and evolves unitarily. The dynamics is generated by a total Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
. The Liouvillian is given by the commutator:
\bm{L}(\cdot)=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H},\cdot]
.

The Hamiltonian is structured as:

\displaystyle\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{H}_{\text{SE}},\quad\hat{H}_{0}=\hat{1}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{H}_{\text{s}}+\hat{H}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{1}_{\text{s}},\quad\hat{H}_{\text{SE}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{U}}}\hat{E}_{k}\otimes\hat{S}_{k},

where
\hat{H}_{0}
represents the non-interacting part of the Hamiltonian, and
\hat{H}_{\text{SE}}
describes the interaction between the subsystem and the environment. Here
\hat{1}_{\text{e}/\text{s}}
are identity operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{e}/\text{s}}
, and
\hat{H}_{\text{s}},\hat{S}_{k}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{s}})
. The environment operators
\hat{H}_{\text{e}},\hat{E}_{k}
are generally unbounded, densely defined operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{e}}
. The system and environment Liouvillians are

\bm{L}_{\text{s}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{s}},\cdot]
,
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{e}},\cdot]
, and the superoperators
\bm{E}_{\alpha},\bm{S}_{\alpha}
are defined from the operators
\hat{E}_{k},\hat{S}_{k}
using left- and right-multiplication superoperators (see
Section

3
for details).

We utilize the induced trace norm for a superoperator
\bm{O}
acting on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
. For a densely defined superoperator
\bm{O}
with domain
\operatorname{Dom}(\bm{O})\subseteq B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
, the norm is defined as:

\|\bm{O}\|_{\text{tr}}:=\sup\{\|\bm{O}(\hat{A})\|_{\text{tr}}:\hat{A}\in\operatorname{Dom}(\bm{O}),\|\hat{A}\|_{\text{tr}}=1\}.

(2.6)

We will repeatedly use the Hölder inequality for operator norms

\|\hat{O}_{1}\hat{O}_{2}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\|\hat{O}_{1}\|\|\hat{O}_{2}\|_{\text{tr}},\quad\hat{O}_{1}\in B(\mathcal{H}),\quad\hat{O}_{2}\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H}).

(2.7)

For instance, for
\bm{O}(\hat{A})=[\hat{O},\hat{A}]
with
\hat{O}\in B(\mathcal{H})
, we have
\|\bm{O}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq 2\left\lVert\hat{O}\right\rVert
. Because
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
is finite-dimensional, each superoperator
\bm{S}_{\alpha}
in
Eq.

2.5
is bounded. Without loss of generality, we may normalize them so that
\|\bm{S}_{\alpha}\|_{\text{tr}}=1
for all
\alpha=1,\cdots,N
.

We start from an uncorrelated initial state:
\hat{\rho}(0)=\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
. The object of interest is the reduced dynamics of the subsystem, obtained by taking the partial trace over the environment:

\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\operatorname{tr}_{\text{E}}(\hat{\rho}(t)).

(2.8)

For any system observable
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
, the time-dependent expectation value of
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
is given by

O_{\text{S}}(t)=\operatorname{tr}(\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)).

(2.9)

Errors in
O_{\text{S}}(t)
are controlled by the errors in
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
and by the operator norm of
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
. The following lemma (a direct consequence of (
2.7
) and the bound
|\operatorname{tr}(\hat{O}_{1})|\leq\|\hat{O}_{1}\|_{\text{tr}}
for trace-class
\hat{O}_{1}
) makes this precise.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
and
O_{\text{S}}(t)
,
O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
be the expectation value of
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
corresponding to two different density operators
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
and
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
, respectively. Then we have

|O_{\text{S}}(t)-O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)|\leq\|\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\|\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}.

(2.10)

If there is no system-environment coupling, i.e.,
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=0
, the total density operator evolves as
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}\hat{\rho}(0)
, where
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}\otimes\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}
. In general,
\hat{\rho}(t)
has the following Dyson’s series expansion for finite
t>0
:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}\hat{\rho}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t-t_{1})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t_{1}}\hat{\rho}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t-t_{1})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t_{2}}\hat{\rho}(0)+\cdots

(2.11)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\iint\cdots\int_{0<t_{n}<\cdots<t_{1}<t}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t-t_{1})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{n-1}-t_{n})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t_{n}}\hat{\rho}(0)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}.

Since
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\bm{E}_{\alpha}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha}
and
\hat{\rho}(0)=\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)
,
Eq.

2.11
is equivalent to the following:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}=1}^{N}\iint\cdots

\displaystyle\int_{0<t_{n}<\cdots<t_{1}<t}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{n}}\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)

(2.12)

\displaystyle\otimes\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t_{n}}\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}.

Eq.

2.11
could be formally verified by taking derivatives on both sides. We will later establish the absolute convergence of the above Dyson series for the specific cases under consideration. Specifically, we verify that the bound given in
Eq.

2.16
holds for the scenarios considered in this work. This bound, in turn, guarantees the absolute convergence of the series in
Eq.

2.12
and
Eq.

2.56
.

To take the partial trace with respect to the environment, it is natural to adopt the following trace-class assumption:

Assumption 2.2

(Environment trace-class assumption)
.

We assume that for all
n
and for any
t\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{n}
, the following operator
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
, known as the
n
-point correlator, is in the trace class:

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{n}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0).

(2.13)

As discussed in detail later, the trace-class assumption is satisfied in all cases studied in this paper, since the initial bath state
\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
is Gaussian and the system-environment coupling is linear. With the trace-class assumption
2.13
, we can define the
n
-point environment superoperator correlation functions
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
(for
t\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{n}\geq 0
) as the trace of the above operators:

Definition 2.3

(
n
-point environment superoperator correlation functions)
.

The
n
-point environment superoperator correlation functions
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
(for
t\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{n}\geq 0
) is defined as the trace of the above operator:

C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})=\operatorname{tr}\left(\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\right).

(2.14)

Note that
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
is independent of
t\in[t_{1},\infty)
since
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}
is a trace preserving map for
t>0
.

Eq.

2.13
directly indicates that the operator

\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{n}}\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\otimes\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t_{n}}\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

is also trace-class for all
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}
and
t\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{n}
, since
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
and
\bm{S}_{\alpha}
are bounded in the induced trace norm. This leads to the following convergence criterion for the Dyson series in
Eq.

2.11
:

Lemma 2.4
.

Fix
t\geq 0
and set

A_{n}(t):=\sup_{t\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{n}\geq 0}\bigl\|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\bigr\|_{\text{tr}},

If

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(Nt)^{n}}{n!}\,A_{n}(t)<+\infty,

(2.15)

then the Dyson series in
Eq.

2.11
(equivalently,
Eq.

2.12
) converges absolutely. Moreover, the summability condition (
2.15
) holds whenever there exists a constant
\widetilde{A}
and
\mathfrak{c}(t)>0
such that

A_{n}(t)\leq\sqrt{n!}\,(\mathfrak{c}(t))^{n}.

(2.16)

Proof.

Taking the trace norm of the right-hand side of
Eq.

2.12
and using
\|\bm{O}_{\text{E}}\otimes\bm{O}_{\text{S}}\|_{\text{tr}}=\|\bm{O}_{\text{E}}\|_{\text{tr}}\|\bm{O}_{\text{S}}\|_{\text{tr}}
yields the first claim. For the second claim, observe that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n}Nt\cdot\mathfrak{c}(t)}{n}=0
. By the ratio test, the summation is therefore finite.
∎

If
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
is finite-dimensional, and thus
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
and
\bm{E}_{\alpha}
are bounded, the estimate
Eq.

2.16
holds naturally. This covers fermionic environments with a finite number of modes. We will show later that this estimate holds for systems of interest in this paper, especially when
\bm{L}_{\text{E}}
and
\bm{E}_{\alpha}
are unbounded, and the scaling in
Eq.

2.16
arises naturally for Gaussian environments and linear system-environment coupling (see
Section

3.1.3
). In particular, for bosonic Gaussian environments, the estimate in
Eq.

2.16
is the direct result of the infrared regularity condition (see
Eq.

3.8
). (See
Eq.

3.19
for the detailed expression of the constant
\mathfrak{c}(t)
.) We note that
Eq.

2.15
guaranties that the
n
-point correlation functions
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
are bounded in the
L^{1}
sense, namely
\int_{[0,T]^{n}}\left|C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\right|\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}<\infty
for all finite
T\geq 0
. One could consider more general correlators such as tempered Radon measures
[
Tri22
,
TR24
]
. Such scenarios, however, fall outside the scope of the systems of interest in this paper.

2.2
Tracing out the environment

Now we are ready to take the partial trace over the environment.

Lemma 2.5
.

For
\hat{\rho}(t)
, if the RHS of
Eq.

2.11
(equivalently,
Eq.

2.12
) is absolutely convergent, then, with the environment trace-class assumption, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}=1}^{N}\iint\cdots

\displaystyle\int_{0<t_{n}<\cdots<t_{1}<t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}

(2.17)

\displaystyle C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t_{n}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

Proof.

The key here is that we have exchanged the partial trace first with the summation and then with the integration. The partial trace, as a linear mapping defined on trace-class operators, is contractive in the trace norm; namely, for any trace-class
\hat{A}
in
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
,
\|\operatorname{tr}_{\text{E}}\hat{A}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\|A\|_{\text{tr}}
. The absolute convergence of the infinite series in
Eq.

2.12
implies the absolute convergence of the RHS of
Eq.

2.17
.
The exchange of the partial trace, summation, and integration is thus justified by the linearity of the partial trace and the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Let us introduce the notation
\bm{S}_{\alpha}(t)
,
\bm{E}_{\alpha}(t)
, and
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}(t)
:

\bm{S}_{\alpha}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\bm{S}_{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t},\quad\bm{E}_{\alpha}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}\bm{E}_{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t},\quad\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\bm{L}_{0}t}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}.

(2.18)

We emphasize that this is just a shorthand notation for convenience since
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}
,
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}
, and
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}
are not necessarily well-defined for
t<0
. Then we could rewrite
Eq.

2.14
and
Eq.

2.17
as:

C_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}^{(n)}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})=\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}(t_{n})\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\right)

(2.19)

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}=1}^{N}\iint\cdots

\displaystyle\int_{0<t_{n}<\cdots<t_{1}<t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}

(2.20)

\displaystyle C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}(t_{n})\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

We emphasize that although
\bm{E}_{\alpha}(t)
and
\bm{S}_{\alpha}(t)
are formal expressions,
Eq.

2.19
and
Eq.

2.20
are well-defined since
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}
(resp.
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}
) appears in front of the sum. For unordered
t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}
, let us define
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
and the time-ordering operation
\mathbf{T}^{\pm}
:

\displaystyle C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})

\displaystyle=(\pm)^{\sigma}C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,\alpha_{\sigma(n)}}(t_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,t_{\sigma(n)}),

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}(t_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=(\pm)^{\sigma}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}(t_{\sigma(1)})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(n)}}(t_{\sigma(n)}),

where
\sigma\in S_{n}
is the permutation of
1,\cdots,n
such that
t_{\sigma(1)}\geq\cdots\geq t_{\sigma(n)}
, and the plus and the minus sign correspond to bosonic and fermionic environments, respectively. Then we can rewrite
Eq.

2.20
as:

\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}=1}^{N}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}(t_{n})\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{n}\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

(2.22)

One can see that, operators under the time-ordering operation (anti-)commute. Let us also introduce the time-ordering operation
\bm{\mathcal{T}}
without the sign change, which will be used in the proof of
Theorem

2.9
, defined as:

\mathcal{\bm{\mathcal{T}}}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}(t_{n})\right)=\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}(t_{\sigma(1)})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(n)}}(t_{\sigma(n)}),

(2.23)

where
\sigma\in S_{n}
is the same permutation as above.

Remark 2.1
.

Note that
C_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}^{(n)}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
is defined using environment superoperators
\bm{E}_{\alpha_{i}}
(
i=1,\cdots,n
), while the commonly-used
n
-point BCF is defined using operators
[
BP07
]
. Nevertheless, we will refer to
C_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}^{(n)}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
as the
n
-point BCF in this section when there are no further confusions.

Remark 2.2
.

For time-ordering operation defined in
Eq.

2.21
, later we will use the following expression

\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right),

in which the time-ordering is understood to be performing on
t_{1},\cdots,t_{n},s_{1},\cdots,s_{k}
. In other words, let
w_{i}=t_{i}
,
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}
(
i=1,\cdots,n
) and
w_{2n+j}=s_{j}
,
\gamma_{2n+j}=\beta_{j}
(
j=1,\cdots,k
), and let
\sigma\in S_{n+k}
be the permutation such that
w_{1}\geq\cdots\geq w_{n+k}
. Then we have
\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right)=(\pm)^{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{n+k}\bm{S}_{\gamma_{i}}(w_{i})
.

In this paper, we consider environment
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
and system-environment coupling
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}
such that the
n
-point BCFs satisfy the following Isserlis-Wick’s condition:

Definition 2.6

(Isserlis-Wick’s conditions for BCFs)
.

For
t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}>0
, the
n
-point BCFs
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
satisfy the Isserlis-Wick’s condition: for
n
odd,
C^{(n)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})=0
, while for
n
even, i.e.,
n=2m
,
we have

C^{(2m)}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2m}}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{2m})=\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{2m}}(\pm)^{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{\alpha_{\sigma(2i-1)},\alpha_{\sigma(2i)}}\left(t_{\sigma(2i-1)},t_{\sigma(2i)}\right),

(2.24)

where
\Pi_{2m}
is the set of pairings of
\{1,\cdots,2m\}
, i.e.,

\Pi_{2m}=\left\{\sigma\in S_{2m}\mid\sigma(2i-1)<\sigma(2i)\text{ for }i\in\{1,\cdots,m\},\text{ and }\sigma(1)<\sigma(3)<\cdots<\sigma(2m-1)\right\}.

Such conditions are known to be satisfied for linearly coupled Gaussian environments in both unitary and non-unitary dynamics. We refer to
Section

3
for examples.
With the Isserlis-Wick’s conditions, we have

Theorem 2.7

(Dyson’s series for
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
after resummation)
.

With the Isserlis-Wick’s conditions in
Definition

2.6
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2m}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2m}=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}\left(t_{1}\right)\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2m}}\left(t_{2m}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}(0).

Proof.

With the definition of
\mathbf{T}^{\pm}
, we have

\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{n}}(t_{n})\right)=(\pm)^{\sigma}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}(t_{\sigma(1)})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(n)}}(t_{\sigma(n)})\right).

Using
Definition

2.6
,
we can rewrite
Eq.

2.22
as:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \hat{{\rho}}_{\text{S}}(t)=&\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2m)!}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2m}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\\
&\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2m}=1}^{N}\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{2m}}(\pm)^{\sigma}\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{\alpha_{\sigma(2i-1)},\alpha_{\sigma(2i)}}(t_{\sigma(2i-1)},t_{\sigma(2i)})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{{2m}}}(t_{{2m}})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)\end{aligned}

(2.26)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}&\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{2m}}\frac{1}{(2m)!}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2m}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\\
&\sum_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)},\cdots,\alpha_{\sigma(2m)}=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{\alpha_{\sigma(2i-1)},\alpha_{\sigma(2i)}}(t_{\sigma(2i-1)},t_{\sigma(2i)})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}(t_{\sigma(1)})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{{\sigma(2m)}}}(t_{{\sigma(2m)}})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).\end{aligned}

By change of variables, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{2m}}\frac{1}{(2m)!}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2m}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2m}=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2m}}(t_{{2m}})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

Since
|\Pi_{2m}|=(2m-1)!!
, we obtain
Eq.

2.25
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

A formal but convenient expression for
Eq.

2.25
, which is known in physics literature (for example,
[
FVJ00
,
BMP04
]
), is:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0),

(2.28)

where the exponential is understood as the formal Taylor’s expansion, and the time-ordering operation
\mathbf{T}^{\pm}
acting on this exponential is understood to be acting on each term of the summation. In other words, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{exp}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\right)

(2.29)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\right)^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int\cdots\int_{\Omega_{n}(t)}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}.

where
\Omega_{n}(t)
is the following region:

\Omega_{n}(t)=\{(t_{1},\cdots,t_{2n})|0<t_{2i}<t_{2i-1}<t,\text{ for }i=1,\cdots,n\}.

Then we have,

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\right)\right)

(2.30)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\int\cdots\int_{\Omega_{n}(t)}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\right)\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}.

We emphasize that the left-hand side of the above equation should only be understood as the shorthand notation for the right-hand side, which is mathematically well-defined.
Because of
Eq.

2.21
,
the right-hand side of
Eq.

2.30
is equal to the right-hand side of
Eq.

2.25
.
Similarly, another shorthand notation for
Eq.

2.25
is
the following:

\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0),

(2.31)

where
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\right)\right)
is understood as the shorthand notation for the following:

\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\right)\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}.

2.3
Grönwall-type error bound

From
Eq.

2.25
, we observe that the reduced system dynamics
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
is fully determined by the system Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
and two-point BCFs
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
. We assume that
(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t})_{t\geq 0}
is a contractive semigroup, which is a natural condition since
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}
describes the (sub)system dynamics governed by the von-Neumann or Lindblad equations (see
Section

3
).
In what follows, we prove the Grönwall-type error bound for
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
under perturbations of
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
.

In the case when
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
depends only on the time difference
(t_{1}-t_{2})
(see
Eq.

2.39
), we introduce the notation
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1}-t_{2})
such that
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{C}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1}-t_{2})
. To differ perturbations in the two-time BCFs
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
and the one-time function
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1}-t_{2})
, we denote the norm of the former by
\delta
for
L^{\infty}
and
\delta_{1}
for
L^{1}
, and the latter by
\epsilon
and
\epsilon_{1}
. The precise definitions of
\delta,\delta_{1},\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}

are given in the following theorem.

Theorem 2.8

(Grönwall-type error bound for reduced system density operators)
.

For the Liouville dynamics
Eq.

2.2
, where the environment and the system-environment coupling satisfies Isserlis-Wick’s condition
Definition

2.6
, consider two open quantum systems: one characterized by the system Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
and two-point BCFs
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
(
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1,\cdots,N
) and the other by the same
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
and perturbed BCFs
C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
. We assume that
(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t})_{t\geq 0}
forms a contractive semigroup.
Let
\hat{\rho}_{S}(t)
and
\hat{\rho}_{S}^{\prime}(t)
be the corresponding reduced system density operators given by
Eq.

2.25
.
Then, given maximal evolution time
T
, the difference
\hat{\rho}_{S}(t)
and
\hat{\rho}_{S}^{\prime}(t)
can be bounded by for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\frac{\delta t^{2}}{2}\mathrm{e}^{Mt^{2}/2},

(2.32)

where
M
and
\delta
are given by:

M=\max\left\{\|C\|_{N,\infty},\|C^{\prime}\|_{N,\infty}\right\},\quad\delta=\left\|C-C^{\prime}\right\|_{N,\infty}.

(2.33)

Here for a
N\times N
matrix-valued function
C
,
we define the norm
\|C\|_{N,\infty}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]^{2})}
.
Similarly, we also have an error estimates in terms of the
L^{1}
norm of the error in the BCF:

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\frac{\delta_{1}t}{2}\mathrm{e}^{M_{1}t/2}.

(2.34)

where
M_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
are defined as

M_{1}=\sup_{t_{2}\in[0,T]}\max\left\{\|C(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{N,1},\|C^{\prime}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{N,1}\right\},\quad\delta=\left\|C(\cdot,t_{2})-C^{\prime}(\cdot,t_{2})\right\|_{N,1},

(2.35)

with
\|C(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{N,1}
defined as
\|C(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{N,1}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}
.

Proof.

Following from
Eq.

2.25
, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2n}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(2.36)

\displaystyle\left|\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right|\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2n}}(t_{2n})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)\|_{\text{tr}}.

Let
\sigma
be the permutation of
1,\cdots,2n
such that
t_{\sigma(1)}\geq\cdots\geq t_{\sigma(2n)}
. Recall that
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}
is a contraction map and
\|\bm{S}_{\alpha}\|_{\text{tr}}=1
, thus

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2n}}(t_{2n})\right)\|_{\text{tr}}=\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t-t_{\sigma(1)})}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t_{\sigma(1)}-t_{\sigma(2)})}\cdots\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(2n)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t_{\sigma(2n)}}\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t-t_{\sigma(1)})}\|_{\text{tr}}\|\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}\|_{\text{tr}}\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t_{\sigma(1)}-t_{\sigma(2)})}\|_{\text{tr}}\cdots\|\bm{S}_{\alpha_{\sigma(2n)}}\|_{\text{tr}}\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t_{\sigma(2n)}}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq 1.

Since
\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)\|_{\text{tr}}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\int_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2n}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\left|\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right|.

Let
\Delta C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}=C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{\prime}-C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}C_{\alpha_{2j-1},\alpha_{2j}}(t_{2j-1},t_{2j})\right)\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\left(\prod_{j=i+1}^{n}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{2j-1},\alpha_{2j}}(t_{2j-1},t_{2j})\right)

Taking absolute value, and summing over
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2N}
, with

\left|\sum_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\right|\leq M
and
\left|\sum_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}=1}^{N}\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\right|\leq\delta
,
we obtain

\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)-\prod_{i=1}^{n}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right|\leq nM^{n-1}\delta.

Then we have:

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}t^{2n}nM^{n-1}\delta=\frac{\delta t^{2}}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{t^{2n-2}M^{n-1}}{2^{n-1}(n-1)!}=\frac{\delta t^{2}}{2}\mathrm{e}^{Mt^{2}/2}.

If instead using the
L_{1}
norm of the BCF, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\Bigg|\prod_{i=1}^{n}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})

\displaystyle-\prod_{i=1}^{n}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})\Bigg|\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}

\displaystyle\leq nM_{1}^{n-1}\delta_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}=t^{n}nM_{1}^{n-1}\delta_{1},

and therefore,

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}t^{n}nM_{1}^{n-1}\delta_{1}=\frac{\delta_{1}t}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n-1}M_{1}^{n-1}}{2^{n-1}(n-1)!}=\frac{\delta_{1}t}{2}\mathrm{e}^{M_{1}t/2}.

∎

Remark 2.4
.

Recall that for
t_{1}\geq t_{2}
,
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
is defined as

C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})=\operatorname{tr}\left({\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{2}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)}\right),\quad t_{1}\geq t_{2}.

If
\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
is a stationary state of
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}
, (i.e.,
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=0
), then
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
(for
t\geq 0
). With the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}
is a trace-preserving map, we have

C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})=\operatorname{tr}\left({\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{2}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)}\right),\quad t_{1}\geq t_{2}.

(2.37)

In other words,
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
relies only on the time difference
(t_{1}-t_{2})
. In such cases, let
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1}-t_{2})=C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
, and let us define
\epsilon
,
\mathcal{M}
,
\epsilon_{1}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\max\left\{\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])},\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\right\},\quad\epsilon=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}-\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}

(2.38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}=\max\left\{\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])},\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}\right\},\quad\epsilon_{1}=\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}-\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}.

\mathcal{C}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}
are single-variable functions, and with comparison to
\delta,M,\delta_{1},M_{1}
defined in
Theorem

2.8
, we have

\delta=\epsilon,\quad M=\mathcal{M},\quad\delta_{1}\leq 2\epsilon_{1},\quad M_{1}\leq 2\mathcal{M}.

As a result, the following error bound holds:

\left\|\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\leq\epsilon_{1}{t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{M}_{1}t},\quad\left\|\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\leq\epsilon\frac{t^{2}}{2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathcal{M}t^{2}/2}.

(2.39)

Remark 2.5
.

As an application of
Eq.

2.10
, an error bound similar to
Eq.

2.39
holds for any bounded system observable
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
. Therefore
Theorem

1.1
is the direct corollary of
Theorem

2.8
using
Eq.

2.10
.

Remark 2.6
.

Note that it could be seen from the definition of
\epsilon
,
\delta
,
\mathcal{M}
,
M
that they may scale quadratically with the system size
N
. This dependence on
N
is natural and expected. However, the primary focus of this work is on the exponential improvement in the
t
-dependence of the error bound, as discussed in
Section

1
, which is the more dominant factor.

2.4
Improved error bound

Now let us discuss the improved error bound. Under the same setup as above, we will assume
(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t})_{t\geq 0}
is a contractive semigroup. Furthermore, let
\bm{L}
,
\bm{L}^{\prime}
denote the total system-environment Liouvillian corresponds to
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
and
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
, respectively.
We also assume that
(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t})_{t\geq 0}
forms a contractive semigroup. Note that we do not impose the same assumption on the perturbed semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}^{\prime}t})_{t\geq 0}
, allowing our analysis applicable to non-contractive frameworks such as quasi-Lindblad formalism (see
Section

4
).

Theorem 2.9

(Main theorem)
.

With the same setup as in
Theorem

2.8
, and
(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t})_{t\geq 0}
is a contractive semigroup,
we have the following improved error bound

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\delta t^{2}/2}-1,

(2.40)

where
\delta
is the difference between
C
and
C^{\prime}
in the
L^{\infty}
sense, as defined in
Eq.

2.33
, while in the
L^{1}
sense, we have

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\delta_{1}t/2}-1,

(2.41)

where
\delta_{1}
is also defined the same as in
Eq.

2.35
.

Remark 2.7
.

Similar to
Eq.

2.39
, in the case that
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
depends only on the time difference
(t_{1}-t_{2})
, we have the following simplified error bound:

\left\|\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon t^{2}/2}-1,\quad\left\|\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{S}}^{\prime}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon_{1}t}-1,

(2.42)

where
\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}
are defined as in
Eq.

2.38
.

Remark 2.8
.

Similar to
Remark

2.5
, using
Eq.

2.10
, the error bound for system observables (see
Theorem

1.2
) follow naturally as the corollary of
Theorem

2.9
.

Prior to giving a rigorous proof for
Theorem

2.9
, let us first introduce a formal yet intuitive argument.

Formal Proof of
Theorem

2.9
.

Let us use the formal exponential formula in
Remark

2.3
.
Formally, all operators (anti-)commute under the time-ordering operation
\mathbf{T}^{\pm}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C^{\prime}_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}}\right)

(2.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}\Delta C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}}\right).

Note that
Eq.

2.43
is a formal expression for now.
Then formally, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}}\right)\right.

(2.44)

\displaystyle\cdot\left.\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}=1}^{N}\Delta C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}}-1\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

A formal expansion on the second exponential in
Eq.

2.44
gives

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})

(2.45)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2})\bm{S}_{\beta_{1}}(t_{1})\bm{S}_{\beta_{2}}(t_{2})\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}}\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

The derivation of
Eq.

2.45
here is formal and will be justified rigorously in
Lemma

2.10
.
We will also prove the following equality in
Lemma

2.11
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2})\bm{S}_{\beta_{1}}(s_{1})\bm{S}_{\beta_{2}}(s_{2})\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}}\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)

(2.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\pm)^{\sigma}\operatorname{tr}_{\text{E}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t-t_{\sigma(1)})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}\prod_{i=2}^{2n}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t_{\sigma(i-1)}-t_{\sigma(i)})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{\sigma(i)}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t_{\sigma(2n)}}\hat{\rho}(0)\right),

where
\sigma\in S_{2n}
is the permutation of
1,\cdots,2n
such that
t_{\sigma(1)}\geq\cdots\geq t_{\sigma(2n)}
,
and
\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha}
is the extension of
\bm{S}_{\alpha}
to the entire extended system. For bosonic environments, we define

\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha}=\bm{1}_{\text{e}}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha}
.
For fermionic systems, we refer to
Appendix

B
for the definition of
\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha}
.
Since the partial trace does not increase the trace norm, the trace norm of LHS of
Eq.

2.46
is bounded by the trace norm for the following:

\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t-t_{\sigma(1)})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}\prod_{i=2}^{2n}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t_{\sigma(i-1)}-t_{\sigma(i)})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{\sigma(i)}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t_{\sigma(2n)}}\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

Furthermore, since
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t}
are contraction mappings for
t\geq 0
, i.e.,
\|\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq 1
, the trace norm of the above is further bounded by

\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\|\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{\sigma(i)}}\|_{\text{tr}}\|\hat{\rho}(0)\|_{\text{tr}}.

(2.47)

In the bosonic case,
\|\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}\|_{\text{t}r}=\|\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\|_{\text{t}r}=1
, and then

Eq.

2.47
is bounded by 1. In the fermionic case, the same error bound holds (see
Appendix

B
).
Now, we have

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}=1}^{N}|\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2i-1},t_{2i})|.

(2.48)

Hence, similar to the proof of
Theorem

2.8
, in the
L^{\infty}
sense, we have
\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta^{n}t^{2n}}{2^{n}n!}=\mathrm{e}^{\delta t^{2}/2}-1
, and in the
L^{1}
sense, we have

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\delta_{1}^{n}t^{n}}{2^{n}n!}=\mathrm{e}^{\delta_{1}t/2}-1
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

We remark that the equality
Eq.

2.46
(proven in
Lemma

2.11
) has been formally derived previously in physics literature in coherent state path integral formalism (for example, see
[
MSHP17
, Eq. 5]
). However, this type of coherent state path integral formalism, aside from the challenges of achieving rigorous justification, has been shown to sometimes yield incorrect results
[
Koc19
]
. In contrast, the derivations presented in this paper, based on the superoperator formalism, are fully valid.

2.5
Proof of Theorem
2.9

To rigorously prove
Theorem

2.9
, we need to mathematically justify
Eq.

2.45
and the equality
Eq.

2.46
.
Let us first prove the former:

Lemma 2.10
.

Eq.

2.45
holds, where the expression

\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}=1}^{N}C_{\beta_{1},\beta_{2}}(s_{1},s_{2})\bm{S}_{\beta_{1}}(s_{1})\bm{S}_{\beta_{2}}(s_{2})\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}}\right)

(2.49)

is understood as shorthand for the following series

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}k!}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{\beta_{2j-1},\beta_{2j}}\left(s_{2j-1},s_{2j}\right)\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right),

(2.50)

where the time-ordering acts jointly on the full list of times
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{2n},s_{1},\ldots,s_{2k}\}
as in
Remark

2.2
.

Proof.

Since
C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}^{\prime}(t_{1},t_{2})=C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})+\Delta C_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(t_{1},t_{2})
, according to
Eq.

2.25
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}(C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}+\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}})\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\sum_{I\subset\{1,\cdots,n\}}\left(\prod_{i\in I}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\prod_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i^{\prime}-1},\alpha_{2i^{\prime}}}\left(t_{2i^{\prime}-1},t_{2i^{\prime}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}\quad(I^{\prime}=\{1,\cdots,n\}\backslash I)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n}n!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2n}=1}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}n\\
k\end{array}\right)\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\prod_{i=k+1}^{n}\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}t_{2n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{2m}\sum_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{2m}=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\Delta C_{\alpha_{2i-1},\alpha_{2i}}\left(t_{2i-1},t_{2i}\right)\right)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k}k!}

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{2j}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{\beta_{2j-1},\beta_{2j}}\left(s_{2j-1},s_{2j}\right)\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2m}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}\left(t_{j}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right).

Here, in the third equality, we have used the following fact: for
t_{1},\cdots,t_{2n}
that are mutually distinct,
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\right)
is invariant after exchanging
\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2i-1}}(t_{2_{i}-1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2i}}(t_{2_{i}})
with
\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2i^{\prime}-1}}(t_{2_{i}^{\prime}-1})\bm{S}_{\alpha_{2i^{\prime}}}(t_{2_{i}^{\prime}})
, for any
i,i^{\prime}\in\{1,\cdots,2n\}
. In the last equality, we exchange the order of summation, which is justified by the dominated convergence theorem since all series summations are absolutely convergent.
Thus,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
could be obtained by replacing
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}
with
\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}
in the above equation,
i.e.,
Eq.

2.45
holds.
∎

Now let us prove the equality
Eq.

2.46
, which would enable us to take advantage of the contractive properties of
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t}
.

Lemma 2.11
.

Under the bounds in
Eq.

2.16
, the identity in
Eq.

2.46
holds, with
Eq.

2.49
interpreted as the shorthand of
Eq.

2.50
(see also
Lemma

2.10
).

Proof of
Lemma

2.11
.

Let us prove both sides of
Eq.

2.46
are equal to the following:

\displaystyle(\pm)^{\sigma_{t}}\operatorname{tr}_{\text{E}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{m}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}(0)\right).

(2.51)

where
\sigma_{t}
is the permutation of
1,\cdots,2n
such that
t_{\sigma_{t}(1)}\geq\cdots\geq t_{\sigma_{t}(2n)}
.
Note that similar to the proof of
Lemma

2.4
, the infinite series in
Eq.

2.51
is absolutely convergent.
For the left-hand side, we have

\displaystyle LHS

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{2^{k}k!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{2k}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}=1}^{N}\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{\beta_{2j-1},\beta_{2j}}\left(s_{2j-1},s_{2j}\right)\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{2k}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{(2k)!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{2k}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\qquad\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{\beta_{\sigma(2j-1)},\beta_{\sigma(2j)}}\left(s_{\sigma(2j-1)},s_{\sigma(2j)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{\sigma(j)}}\left(s_{\sigma({j})}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\sigma\in\Pi_{2k}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{(2k)!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{2k}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}=1}^{N}(\pm)^{\sigma}

\displaystyle\qquad\prod_{j=1}^{k}C_{\beta_{\sigma(2j-1)},\beta_{\sigma(2j)}}\left(s_{\sigma(2j-1)},s_{\sigma(2j)}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{(2k)!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{2k}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}=1}^{N}C^{(2k)}_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{2k}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{2k})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2k}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{m!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{m}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m}=1}^{N}C^{(m)}_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

Here, we have used the Isserlis-Wick’s conditions (see
Definition

2.6
) for
m
-point BCFs
C^{(m)}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m})=\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{E}_{\beta_{j}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\right)
. Then, we only need to prove

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{m!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{m}\sum_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m}=1}^{N}C^{(m)}_{\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m}}(s_{1},\cdots,s_{m})\mathbf{T}^{\pm}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0).

(2.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\pm)^{\sigma_{t}}\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{E}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{\penalty\ d}s_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{m}\bm{\mathcal{T}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{L}_{\mathrm{SE}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}(0)\right).

For bosonic cases, we further have

\mathbf{T}^{+}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{e}}\left(t_{i}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{E}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=\mathbf{T}^{+}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{E}_{\beta_{j}}\left(s_{j}\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0).

Here,
\bm{1}_{\text{e}}(t)=\bm{1}_{\text{e}}
and the time
t
serves as the time argument while performing time-ordering.
The above equality holds since
{\mathbf{T}}^{+}
is the time-ordering operation without adding any additional sign. We have

\displaystyle\text{LHS of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:the_thing_need_to_be_proved}}

(2.53)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}}{m!}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{m}

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}\left(\mathbf{T}^{+}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{1}_{\text{e}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{E}_{\beta_{j}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\right)\mathbf{T}^{+}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\bm{S}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{S}_{\beta_{j}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{RHS of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:the_thing_need_to_be_proved}}.

The above derivation is similar to reversing the proof of
Eq.

2.25
, and we also have used that

|\Pi_{2k}|=(2k-1)!!
,
\mathrm{e}^{L_{\text{e}}t}
is a trace-preserving map, and the Isserlis-Wick’s condition (see
Definition

2.6
). During this process, we have also exchanged the partial trace and integral, which is justified by the dominated convergence theorem, similar as in the proof of
Eq.

2.17
.
In the fermionic case, one needs to pay extra attention to the additional sign arising from the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded tensor product structure (see
Appendix

A
).
We leave the proof of
Eq.

2.52
in the fermionic case to
Appendix

B
.

For RHS of
Eq.

2.46
, without loss of generality, let us assume that
t_{2n}\geq t_{2n-1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{1}
.
Comparing
Eq.

2.51
and the RHS of
Eq.

2.46
, we only need to prove that

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{m}\bm{\mathcal{T}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}(t_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{m}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}(s_{j})\right)\hat{\rho}(0)

(2.54)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t-t_{2n})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{2n}}\prod_{i=2}^{2n}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i-1}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t_{1}}\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

Since LHS of
Eq.

2.54
is absolutely convergent, we have

LHS of
Eq.

2.54

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}t}\left(\sum_{m_{2n}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m_{2n}!}\int_{t_{2n}}^{t}\mathrm{\penalty\ d}s_{1}^{(2n)}\cdots\int_{t_{2n}}^{t}\mathrm{\penalty\ d}s_{m_{2n}}^{(2n)}\bm{\mathcal{T}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2n}}\bm{L}_{\mathrm{SE}}\left(s_{i}^{(2n)}\right)\right)\right)\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{2n}}(t_{2n})

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{m_{2n-1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m_{2n-1}!}\int_{t_{2n-1}}^{t_{2n}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(2n-1)}\cdots\int_{t_{2n-1}}^{t_{2n}}\mathrm{d}s_{m_{2n-1}}^{({2n-1})}\bm{\mathcal{T}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{2n-1}}\bm{L}_{\mathrm{SE}}\left(s_{i}^{({2n-1})}\right)\right)\right)\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{2n-1}}(t_{2n-1})\cdots

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{m_{1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m_{1}!}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(1)}\cdots\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{d}s_{m_{1}}^{(1)}\bm{\mathcal{T}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{1}}\bm{L}_{\mathrm{SE}}\left(s_{i}^{(1)}\right)\right)\right)\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\sum_{m_{0}=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m_{0}!}\int_{0}^{t_{2n}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(0)}\cdots\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}s_{m_{0}}^{(0)}\bm{\mathcal{T}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m_{0}}\bm{L}_{\mathrm{SE}}\left(s_{i}^{(0)}\right)\right)\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

(2.55)

Here we have used the following combinatorial equality: for
m=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{2n+1}
,

\frac{1}{m!}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}m\\
m_{1}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}m-m_{1}\\
m_{2}\end{array}\right)\cdots\left(\begin{array}[]{c}m-m_{1}-\cdots-m_{2n}\\
m_{2n+1}\end{array}\right)=\frac{1}{m_{1}!}\cdots\frac{1}{m_{2n+1}!}.

Proceeding further, using the definition of the time-ordering operator, we see that
Eq.

2.55
is equivalent to the following equality, for all
k\geq 0
, and all
\alpha_{i}=1,\cdots,N
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t-t_{k})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{k}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t_{k}-t_{k-1})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{k-1}}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t_{2}-t_{1})}\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}t_{1}}\hat{\rho}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{0},n_{1},\cdots,n_{k}=0}^{\infty}\int_{t\geq s_{n_{k}}^{(k)}\geq\cdots\geq s_{1}^{(k)}\geq t_{k}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(k)}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{n_{k}}^{(k)}\int_{t_{k}\geq s_{n_{k-1}}^{(k-1)}\geq\cdots\geq s_{1}^{(k-1)}\geq t_{k-1}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(k-1)}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{n_{k-1}}^{(k-1)}

\displaystyle\cdots\int_{t_{1}\geq s_{n_{0}}^{(0)}\geq\cdots\geq s_{1}^{(0)}\geq t_{0}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(0)}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{n_{0}}^{(0)}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t-s_{n_{k}}^{(k)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{k}}^{(k)}-s_{n_{k}-1}^{(k)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}^{(k)}-t_{k})}\right)\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{k}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{k}-s_{n_{k-1}}^{(k-1)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{k-1}}^{(k-1)}-s_{n_{k-1}-1}^{(k-1)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}^{(k-1)}-t_{k-1})}\right)\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{k-1}}\cdots

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{2}-s_{n_{1}}^{(1)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{1}}^{(1)}-s_{n_{1}-1}^{(1)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}^{(1)}-t_{1})}\right)\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{1}-s_{n_{0}}^{(0)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{0}}^{(0)}-s_{n_{0}-0}^{(0)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{0}^{(0)})}\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

(2.56)

Formally, this is to justify the replacement of
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})}
with the following for all
i
:

\sum_{n_{i}=0}^{\infty}\int_{t_{i+1}\geq s_{n_{i}}^{(i)}\geq\cdots\geq s_{1}^{(i)}\geq t_{i}}\mathrm{d}s_{1}^{(i)}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{n_{i}}^{(i)}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{i}-s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)}-s_{n_{i-1}-1}^{(i-1)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}^{(i-1)}-t_{i-1})}\right).

Recall that given
\hat{\rho}\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
, the following equality holds:

\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}(t-s)}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{t\geq s_{n}\geq\cdots\geq s_{1}\geq s}\mathrm{d}s_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{n}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t-s_{n})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n}-s_{n-1})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}-s)}\hat{\rho},

under the condition that the RHS is absolutely convergent in trace norm. Therefore the proof of
Eq.

2.56
rests on verifying the RHS is absolutely convergent under trace norm. This property is guaranteed by the estimate
Eq.

2.16
. We refer to the following
Lemma

2.12
for the details of this calculation.

∎

Lemma 2.12
.

Under the bounds in
Eq.

2.16
,
Eq.

2.56
holds for all
k\geq 0
, and all
\alpha_{i}=1,\cdots,N
; moreover, the infinite series on the RHS of
Eq.

2.56
is absolutely convergent under trace norm.

Proof of
Lemma

2.12
.

We will prove this by induction. For
k=0
,
Eq.

2.56
reduces to
Eq.

2.11
, for which we have established the absolute convergence using the estimate
Eq.

2.16
. Assume that we have already shown that the lemma holds for
k=k_{0}-1
. In this case, it suffices to show that the RHS of
Eq.

2.56
is absolutely convergent when
k=k_{0}
. Let us derive the upper bound of the trace norm for the following:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{k}}

\displaystyle(t,t_{1:k},s_{1:n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,s_{1:n_{k}}^{(k)})=\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t-s_{n_{k}}^{(k)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{k}}^{(k)}-s_{n_{k}-1}^{(k)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}^{(k)}-t_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha_{i}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(t_{i}-s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)})}\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)}-s_{n_{i-1}-1}^{(i-1)})}\cdots\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{0}(s_{1}^{(i-1)}-t_{i-1})}\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

In the fermionic case, since both
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
is finite-dimensional,
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}
are bounded (say by a constant
\mathfrak{c}
), and recall that
\|\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha}\|=1
, thus

\|\hat{\rho}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{k}}(t,t_{1:k},s_{1:n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,s_{1:n_{k}}^{(k)})\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k}}.

Then we have

\|\text{ RHS of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:expansion_longest}}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sum_{n_{0},\cdots,n_{k}=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{c}^{n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k}}\frac{(t-t_{k})^{n_{k}}}{n_{k}!}\cdots\frac{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n_{1}}}{n_{1}!}\frac{t_{1}^{n_{0}}}{n_{0}!}=\prod_{i=0}^{k}\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{c}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})}<+\infty.

Now let us focus on the bosonic case, in which
\widetilde{\bm{S}}_{\alpha}=\bm{1}_{\text{E}}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha}
. Using
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{N}E_{\beta}\otimes S_{\beta}
, we have:

\hat{\rho}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{k}}(t,t_{1:k},s_{1:n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,s_{1:n_{k}}^{(k)})=\sum_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)}=1}^{N}\cdots\sum_{\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}=1}^{N}\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}\otimes\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-s_{n_{k}}^{(k)})}\bm{E}_{\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(s_{n_{k}}^{(k)}-s_{n_{k}-1}^{(k)})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\beta_{1}^{(k)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(s_{1}^{(k)}-t_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{i}-s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)})}\bm{E}_{\beta_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)}-s_{n_{i-1}-1}^{(i-1)})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\beta_{1}^{(i-1)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(s_{1}^{(i-1)}-t_{i-1})}\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

\displaystyle\mathcal{\hat{S}}_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t-s_{n_{k}}^{(k)})}\bm{S}_{\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(s_{n_{k}}^{(k)}-s_{n_{k}-1}^{(k)})}\cdots\bm{S}_{\beta_{1}^{(k)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(s_{1}^{(k)}-t_{k})}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(t_{i}-s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)})}\bm{S}_{\beta_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(s_{n_{i-1}}^{(i-1)}-s_{n_{i-1}-1}^{(i-1)})}\cdots\bm{S}_{\beta_{1}^{(i-1)}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}(s_{1}^{(i-1)}-t_{i-1})}\right)\hat{\rho}(0).

Since
\|\bm{S}_{\beta}\|=1
, we have
\|\mathcal{\hat{S}}_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq 1
. Using the estimate
Eq.

2.16
, we have the following bound:

\|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\beta_{1}^{(0)},\cdots\beta_{n_{0}}^{(0)},\cdots,\beta_{1}^{(k)},\cdots\beta_{n_{k}}^{(k)}}\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sqrt{(n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k})!}(\mathfrak{c}(t))^{n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k}}.

Therefore we could bound the total infinite series as follows:

\displaystyle\|\text{ RHS of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq:expansion_longest}}\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n_{0},\cdots,n_{k}=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{(n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k})!}\mathfrak{c}(t)^{n_{0}+\cdots+n_{k}}\frac{(t-t_{k})^{n_{k}}}{n_{k}!}\cdots\frac{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n_{1}}}{n_{1}!}\frac{t_{1}^{n_{0}}}{n_{0}!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{m!}(\mathfrak{c}(t))^{m}\frac{((t-t_{k})+\cdots+(t_{2}-t_{1})+t_{1})^{m}}{m!}=\sum_{m=0^{\infty}}\frac{(t\mathfrak{c}(t))^{m}}{\sqrt{m!}}<+\infty.

∎

3
Applications to spin-boson and fermionic models described by unitary and Lindblad dynamics

In this section, we develop the error estimates for spin-boson and fermionic impurity models, under unitary dynamics and Lindblad dynamics, respectively. We will also discuss quasi-Lindblad pseuodomode theory in the form of
[
PHZ+24
]
in
Section

4
. The differences in terms of the structure of the Liouvillians are shown in
Table

1
.

Environment Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}

System-environment Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}

Unitary
