\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{e}},\cdot]

\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{SE}},\cdot]

Lindblad

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}

\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{SE}},\cdot]

Quasi-Lindblad

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}

\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}

Table 1:
Comparison of Liouvillian forms in unitary, Lindblad, and quasi-Lindblad dynamics.
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
is the environment Liouvillian where
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
and
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
are the environment Liouvillians from the environment Hamiltonian and environment dissipator, respectively, as defined in
Eq.

3.3
for spin-boson models and in
Eq.

3.33
and
Eq.

3.36
for fermionic impurity models.
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}
is the system-environment coupling Liouvillian where
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
and
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
are the system-environment coupling Liouvillian from the system-environment Hamiltonian and system-environment dissipator, respectively, defined in
Eq.

3.7
,
Eq.

4.2
for spin-boson models and
Eq.

3.43
and
Eq.

4.4
for fermionic impurity models.

This section is organized as follows. The spin-boson model and the fermionic impurity model are discussed in
Section

3.1
and
Section

3.2
, respectively. In each case, we first discuss the bosonic/fermionic environment in
Section

3.1.1
and
Section

3.2.1
, in which we give superoperator formalisms for the unitary and non-unitary environment.
Then, we describe the system-environment interaction in
Section

3.1.2
and
Section

3.2.2
.
Finally, we discuss the reduced system dynamics
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
and the error bound in
Section

3.1.4
and
Section

3.2.3
,
as a direct application of
Eq.

2.25
and
Theorem

2.9
.

3.1
Spin-boson model

3.1.1
Bosonic environment

Let
N_{\text{e}}
be the number of environment modes.
For bosonic environments, let
\hat{b}_{k},\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
be the bosonic annihilation and creation operators for the
k
-th environment modes (
k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}
), which satisfy the canonical commutation relation (CCR)
[\hat{b}_{k},\hat{b}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}]=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\hat{1}_{\text{e}}
.
\hat{1}_{\text{e}}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}})
means the identity operator.
Let us introduce the following environment superoperators, which are defined by multiplication of
\hat{b}_{k},\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
to an operator in
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}})
from the left and from the right:

Definition 3.1

(Bosonic environment superoperators)
.

For bosonic environment operators
\hat{b}_{k},\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
(
k=1,\cdots,{N_{\text{e}}}
),
let us define the following environment superoperators, denoted as

\bm{b}_{k}
,
\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
:

\bm{b}_{k}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{b}_{k}(\cdot),\quad\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}(\cdot),\quad\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}:(\cdot)\rightarrow(\cdot)\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\quad\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow(\cdot)\hat{b}_{k}.

(3.1)

\widetilde{\bm{O}}
indicates the operation that multiplies operator
\hat{O}^{\dagger}
from the right.

Remark 3.1
.

Note that in
Definition

3.1
,
\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
is not the conjugate of
\bm{b}_{k}
as an operator on
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}})
. Moreover, we define
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
to be multiplying
\hat{b}_{k}
(not
\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
) from the right so that the usual commutation relation holds:

\left(\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{l}^{\dagger}-\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}\right)\hat{\rho}=\hat{\rho}\hat{b}_{l}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\hat{\rho}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{l}=\hat{\rho}\delta_{kl}.

Let us summarize the CCR in the following lemma:

Lemma 3.2

(Canonical commutation relations (CCR) for bosonic environment superoperators)
.

The superoperators
\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
(
k,k^{\prime}=1,\cdots,{N_{\text{e}}}
) satisfy the following canonical commutation relations:

\displaystyle\left[\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\bm{1}_{\text{e}},\quad\left[\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\bm{1}_{\text{e}},\quad

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left[\bm{b}_{k},{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]=\left[\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]=\left[\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]=\left[\bm{b}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]=\left[\bm{b}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]=\left[\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right]=0.

In this paper, we will use the following two bosonic Liouvillians: a Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
from environment Hamiltonian and a Liouvillian
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
from the environment Lindblad dissipator:

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}\hat{\rho}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{e}},\hat{\rho}],\quad\bm{D}_{\text{e}}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{e}}\Gamma_{kl}\left(2\hat{b}_{l}\hat{\rho}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{l}\hat{\rho}-\hat{\rho}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{l}\right),

where
\hat{H}_{\text{e}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}H_{kl}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{l}
is the environment Hamiltonian.
H
is a
N_{\mathrm{e}}\times N_{\mathrm{e}}
Hermitian matrix and
\Gamma
is a
N_{\mathrm{e}}\times N_{\mathrm{e}}
positive semi-definite matrix. In the general case,
H

and
\Gamma
are not diagonal matrices
[
LGVF24
]
.
In the superoperator representation,
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
and
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
are expressed as

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}-\mathrm{i}H_{kl}\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\bm{b}_{l}+\mathrm{i}H_{kl}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\quad\bm{D}_{\mathrm{e}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{\mathrm{e}}}\Gamma_{kl}\left(2\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}\bm{b}_{l}-\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\bm{b}_{l}-\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}\right).

(3.3)

The goal of the pseudomode theory is to simulate the reduced density dynamics
by replacing the unitary environment with the non-unitary pseudomode environment. The Liouvillian form for the unitary environment is given by
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
. For the unitary dynamics, we consider the initial environment density operator
\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
given by a Gibbs state:

\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=\frac{1}{Z_{\text{e}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\hat{H}_{\text{e}}},\quad Z_{\text{e}}=\operatorname{tr}(\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\hat{H}_{\text{e}}}),

(3.4)

for some inverse temperature
\beta>0
. For the pseudomode environment, the Liouvillian form is given by
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
and we follow the initial density operator setting in
[
TSHP18
,
MSS+20
,
PHZ+24
]
to consider also the initial vacuum environment:

\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=|0\rangle_{\text{E}}\langle 0|_{\text{E}},

(3.5)

where
|0\rangle_{\text{E}}
is the environment vacuum state.
Adapting the following analysis to different initial pseudomode density operators (e.g., in
[
LWvDG20
,
BMAP+20
,
WERZ20
]
) is straightforward. Note that both
Eq.

3.4
and
Eq.

3.5
satisfy
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=0
, and
Eq.

3.5
satisfies that
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=0
. As a result, as discussed in
Eq.

2.39
, the two-point BCFs
C_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})
rely only on
(t-t^{\prime})
for both unitary and pseudomode cases.

3.1.2
System-environment coupling

In the spin-boson model, the system is made up of
n
spins. The system Hilbert space is
\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}=\otimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
Let
\hat{\sigma}_{x}^{(j)},\hat{\sigma}_{y}^{(j)},\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
be Pauli matrices acting on the
j
-th spin, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{a}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=(\otimes_{i=1}^{j-1}\hat{\sigma}_{0})\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{a}\otimes(\otimes_{i=j+1}^{n}\hat{\sigma}_{0}),\quad a\in\{x,y,z\}.

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{x}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\hat{\sigma}_{y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\hat{\sigma}_{z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Similar to
Definition

3.1
, for
\hat{\sigma}_{0}^{(j)}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{x,y,z}^{(j)}
, let us define system superoperators
\bm{\sigma}_{a}^{(j)}
,
\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{a}^{(j)}
for
a=0,x,y,z
:

\bm{\sigma}_{a}^{(j)}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{\sigma}_{a}^{(j)}(\cdot),\quad\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{a}^{(j)}:(\cdot)\rightarrow(\cdot)\hat{\sigma}_{a}^{(j)}.

(3.6)

For
j=1,\cdots,n
, let us define
\bm{S}_{2j-1}=\bm{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}
,
\bm{S}_{2j}=\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{z}^{(j)}
.
We consider a system-environment coupling Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
given by
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{SE}},\cdot]
, where
\hat{H}_{\text{SE}}
is the system-environment Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{SE}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{e}}}\left(g_{jk}\hat{b}_{k}+g_{jk}^{*}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\right)\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}
.
g
is a
n\times N_{\mathrm{e}}
complex-valued matrix and is the coupling coefficient matrix for the system-environment coupling.
We decompose the coupling Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
in terms of system and environment superoperator:

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2n}\bm{E}_{\alpha}^{\text{U}}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha},\quad\bm{E}^{\text{U}}_{2j-1}=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}(g_{jk}\bm{b}_{k}+g_{jk}^{*}\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}),\quad\bm{E}^{\text{U}}_{2j}=\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}(g_{jk}^{*}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}+g_{jk}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}).

(3.7)

To establish the growth estimate described in
Eq.

2.16
, we require the following condition on matrices
H
and
g
, which is a natural physical condition
[
Dav81
]
known as
infrared regularity
:

Assumption 3.3

(Discrete infrared regularity condition)
.

Assume that all rows of matrix
g
are non-zero.
For the matrix
H
in environment Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{e}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}H_{kl}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{l}
and system-environment coupling
g
defined in
Section

3.1.2
, there exists a constant
c_{\text{J}}>0
such that

c_{\text{J}}H\succeq g^{\dagger}g,

(3.8)

Here we use the notation
A\succeq B
to mean that
A-B
is a positive semi-definite matrix.

Example 3.4
.

Let us use the example of a discretized Ohmic bath coupled to a single spin to illustrate the infrared regularity condition. For simplicity, let us consider a uniform bath discretization, though similar results hold for discretization based on other quadrature rules. Let
\omega_{k}=\frac{k}{N_{\text{e}}}\omega_{\text{M}}
for
k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}
. Here
\omega_{\text{M}}>0
is the maximum cutoff frequency. Then
g_{k}
is given by
g_{k}=\sqrt{\frac{\omega_{\text{M}}}{N_{\text{e}}}}\cdot\sqrt{\omega_{k}\mathrm{e}^{-\omega_{k}/\omega_{\text{c}}}}
, where
\omega_{\text{c}}>0
is the characteristic frequency. This is because
|g_{k}|^{2}
comes from discretizing the Ohmic spectral density
J(\omega)=\omega\mathrm{e}^{-\omega/\omega_{\text{c}}}
on the interval
[0,\omega_{\text{M}}]
, and the factor
\frac{\omega_{\text{M}}}{N_{\text{e}}}
is the corresponding quadrature weight.

In this case, the environment Hamiltonian matrix
H
is a diagonal matrix with
H_{kk}=\omega_{k}
, and
g^{\dagger}g
is also a diagonal matrix with
(g^{\dagger}g)_{kk}=|g_{k}|^{2}=\frac{\omega_{\max}}{N_{\text{e}}}\cdot\omega_{k}\mathrm{e}^{-\omega_{k}/\omega_{\text{c}}}
. Therefore, we have

c_{\text{J}}H\succeq g^{\dagger}g,\quad c_{\text{J}}=\frac{\omega_{\max}}{N_{\text{e}}}.

(3.9)

Note that the
\frac{1}{N_{\text{e}}}
scaling of
c_{\text{J}}
is a direct consequence of the quadrature weight from discretization, and does not rely on the Ohmic nature of the bath.

3.1.3
Growth estimate of environment correlators

Let us recall the environment superoperator correlators defined in
Eq.

2.13
:

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{n}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0).

We now establish the growth estimate described in
Eq.

2.16
. Let us start with the following useful inequality for a single bosonic mode:

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
\hat{E}
be a monomial in
\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}^{\dagger}
of total degree
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
|m\rangle
be the
m
-particle state, i.e.,
\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}|m\rangle=m|m\rangle
. Then for all
m\geq 0
, we have

\|\hat{E}|m\rangle\|^{2}\leq\frac{(m+n)!}{m!}.

(3.10)

Proof.

We will use induction on
n
. For
n=1
,
\|\hat{b}|m\rangle\rangle\|^{2}=m
and
\|\hat{b}^{\dagger}|m\rangle\|^{2}=m+1
, so the proposition holds. Now suppose the proposition holds for degree
n-1
. Let
\hat{E}=\hat{B}\hat{E}^{\prime}
, where
\hat{B}
is either
\hat{b}
or
\hat{b}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{E}^{\prime}
is of degree
(n-1)
. Let
|\psi\rangle=\hat{E}^{\prime}|m\rangle
. By the induction hypothesis, we have
\|\psi\|^{2}\leq(m+n-1)!/m!
.

Let
m^{\prime}
be the particle number of
|\psi\rangle
. Since
\hat{E}^{\prime}
is of degree
(n-1)
, thus
m^{\prime}\leq m+n-1
. If
\hat{B}=\hat{b}
, then
\|\hat{E}|\psi\rangle\|^{2}=m^{\prime}\|\psi\|^{2}
. If
\hat{B}=\hat{b}^{\dagger}
, then
\|\hat{E}|\psi\rangle\|^{2}=(m^{\prime}+1)\|\psi\|^{2}
. In both cases, we have
\|\hat{E}|\psi\rangle\|^{2}\leq(m^{\prime}+1)\|\psi\|^{2}\leq(m+n)\|\psi\|^{2}\leq(m+n)(m+n-1)!/m!=(m+n)!/m!
. This completes the induction.
∎

For
\omega>0
, the thermal state is given by
\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}_{\beta,\omega}=(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega})\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-m\beta\omega}|m\rangle\langle m|
. Here
\beta\in(0,+\infty]
. For
\beta=+\infty
,
\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega)=|0\rangle\langle 0|
is the vacuum state. Let us also define the thermal factor
c_{\beta}(\omega)=(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega})^{-1/2}
. For
\beta=+\infty
, we have
c_{\beta}(\omega)=1
. Using
Eq.

3.10
, we could establish the following estimate:

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
\hat{E}
be a monomial in
\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}^{\dagger}
of total degree
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we have

\operatorname{tr}(\hat{E}^{\dagger}\hat{E}\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}_{\beta,\omega})\leq n!c_{\beta}(\omega)^{2n}.

(3.11)

Proof.

Using
Eq.

3.10
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(\hat{E}^{\dagger}\hat{E}\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}_{\beta,\omega})

\displaystyle=(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega})\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-m\beta\omega}\|\hat{E}|m\rangle\|^{2}\leq(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega})\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-m\beta\omega}\frac{(m+n)!}{m!}

Using the binomial identity
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{m+n}{n}x^{m}=(1-x)^{-(n+1)}
for
|x|<1
, the above sum is equal to
n!(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega})^{-n}=n!c_{\beta}(\omega)^{2n}
.
∎

Now let us establish the following inequality for superoperators acting on the thermal state:

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\bm{B}_{1},\cdots,\bm{B}_{n}\in\{\bm{b},\bm{b}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}},\widetilde{\bm{b}}^{\dagger}\}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\bm{b}
,
\bm{b}^{\dagger}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}^{\dagger}
are defined in
Eq.

3.1
meaning multiplications of
\hat{b}
,
\hat{b}^{\dagger}
from the left and from the right. Then we have

\|\bm{B}_{1}\cdots\bm{B}_{n}(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sqrt{n!}c_{\beta}(\omega)^{n}.

(3.12)

Proof.

The resulting operator
\bm{B}_{1}\cdots\bm{B}_{n}(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))
could be written as
(\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))\hat{B}_{\mathrm{R}})
, where
\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}
and
\hat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}
are monomials in
\hat{b}
and
\hat{b}^{\dagger}
corresponding to the left and right multiplications. Let
n_{\mathrm{L}}
and
n_{\mathrm{R}}
be their degrees, respectively. Then
n_{\mathrm{L}}+n_{\mathrm{R}}=n
.

Let
Y=\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))^{1/2}
and
Z=(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))^{1/2}\hat{B}_{\mathrm{R}}
. We calculate the Hilbert-Schmidt norms of
Y
and
Z
. We have
\|Y\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}=\operatorname{tr}((\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))^{1/2}\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\dagger}\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))^{1/2})=\operatorname{tr}(\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\dagger}\hat{B}_{\mathrm{L}}\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))
. By
Eq.

3.11
, this is bounded by
n_{\mathrm{L}}!c_{\beta}(\omega)^{2n_{\mathrm{L}}}
. Similarly, we have
\|Z\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}\leq n_{\mathrm{R}}!c_{\beta}(\omega)^{2n_{\mathrm{R}}}
.

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
\|YZ\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\|Y\|_{\text{HS}}\|Z\|_{\text{HS}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{B}_{1}\cdots\bm{B}_{n}(\hat{\rho}^{\text{th}}(\omega))\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle=\|YZ\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\|Y\|_{\text{HS}}\|Z\|_{\text{HS}}\leq\sqrt{n_{\mathrm{L}}!n_{\mathrm{R}}!}c_{\beta}(\omega)^{n_{\mathrm{L}}+n_{\mathrm{R}}}\leq\sqrt{n!}c_{\beta}(\omega)^{n}.

∎

Without loss of generality, let us assume that the environment Hamiltonian matrix
H
is diagonal, i.e.,
H_{kl}=\omega_{k}\delta_{kl}
. Otherwise, one can always diagonalize
H
with
\tilde{H}=U^{\dagger}HU
and changing the coupling matrix accordingly as
\tilde{g}=gU
.
We also note that for a dissipative bosonic environment, we can assume that
H
is diagonal; however, we cannot assume that both
H
and
\Gamma
are diagonal simultaneously. With the diagonal assumption of
H
, the initial environment density operator
Eq.

3.4
could be written as

\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega_{k}}\right)\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-m\beta\omega_{k}}|m\rangle_{k}\langle m|_{k},

(3.13)

Note that from the infrared regularity condition
Eq.

3.8
, we have
c_{\text{J}}\omega_{k}\geq(g^{\dagger}g)_{kk}=(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\text{s}}}|g_{jk}|^{2})
. Thus all
\omega_{k}
’s are strictly positive and
c_{\beta}(\omega_{k})
is well-defined.
Then we have the following multi-mode growth estimate:

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\bm{B}_{1},\cdots,\bm{B}_{n}\in\{\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\}_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. With the initial environment density operator given by
Eq.

3.13
, we have

\|\bm{B}_{1}\cdots\bm{B}_{n}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\prod_{k=1}^{n}\sqrt{n_{k}!}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k})^{n_{k}},

(3.14)

where
n_{k}
is the total number of superoperators in
\{\bm{B}_{1},\cdots,\bm{B}_{n}\}
that belong to
\{\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\}
.

Proof.

For
\bm{B}_{1},\ldots,\bm{B}_{n}\in\{\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\}_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}
, we can rewrite
\bm{B}_{1}\cdots\bm{B}_{n}(\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0))
as
\bigotimes_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}(\bm{B}^{(k)}(\hat{\rho}^{k,\text{th}}_{\beta,\omega}))
, where
\bm{B}^{(k)}
is a monomial in
\bm{b}_{k}
,
\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
of degree
n_{k}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}n_{k}=n
, and
\hat{\rho}^{k,\text{th}}_{\beta,\omega}
is the thermal state for the
k
-th mode. Then using the single-mode estimate in
Eq.

3.12
, we have

\|\bm{B}^{(k)}(\hat{\rho}^{k,\text{th}}_{\beta,\omega})\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sqrt{n_{k}!}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k})^{n_{k}}
. Multiplying these estimates for all
k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}
gives the desired result.
∎

We will use that
\bm{E}_{\alpha}
’s are linear combinations of the bosonic environment superoperators with bounded coefficients. Let

\bm{b}_{k,1}=\bm{b}_{k}
,
\bm{b}_{k,2}=\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
,
\bm{b}_{k,3}=\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}
,
\bm{b}_{k,4}=\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
, then
\bm{E}_{\alpha}
is of the following form

\bm{E}_{\alpha}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\sum_{s=1}^{4}p_{\alpha,(k,s)}\bm{b}_{k,s},\quad\alpha=1,\cdots,N,\quad|p_{\alpha,(k,s)}|<+\infty.

(3.15)

To capture the system-environment coupling strength for bath mode
k
, let us define
C_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}
(
k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}
) as

C_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}=\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{s=1}^{4}|p_{\alpha,(k,s)}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(3.16)

For the spin-boson model described in
Section

3.1.2
, we have
(C^{\text{int}}_{k})^{2}=2\sum_{j=1}^{n}|g_{jk}|^{2}=2(g^{\dagger}g)_{kk}
. Using the infrared regularity condition
c_{\text{J}}H\succeq g^{\dagger}g
(see
Eq.

3.8
) and the diagonal assumption
H_{kk^{\prime}}=\omega_{k}\delta_{kk^{\prime}}
, we have

C^{\text{int}}_{k}\leq\sqrt{2c_{\text{J}}\omega_{k}}.\quad\text{for }k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}.\quad\text{(from infrared regularity condition)}

(3.17)

Lemma 3.9

(Growth estimate for
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
)
.

Consider the unitary or pseudomode spin-boson dynamics in
Section

3.1
with initial state
Eq.

3.13
and the infrared regularity condition in
Assumption

3.3
.
Then there exists a constant
\mathfrak{c}
(for unitary environment, depending on
N_{\text{e}}
,
c_{\text{J}}
,
\beta
,
\omega_{\text{M}}
, and for dissipative environment, further depending on
t
) such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any choice of
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}\in\{1,\cdots,N\}
, we have the following estimate for
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{n}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
:

\|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sqrt{n!}\mathfrak{c}^{n}.

(3.18)

We will show that for unitary environment, the constant
\mathfrak{c}
could be chosen as follows for all
t\geq 0
:

\mathfrak{c}=2\sqrt{2}N_{\text{e}}c_{\text{J}}\cdot\max\{\sqrt{\omega_{\text{M}}},\beta^{-1/2}\},

(3.19)

where
N_{\text{e}}
is the number of environment modes,
c_{\text{J}}
is the constant in the infrared regularity condition
Eq.

3.8
,
\omega_{\text{M}}=\max_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\omega_{k}
is the maximum environment frequency. For dissipative environment, there is a further exponential time dependence in
\mathfrak{c}
.

We also remark that in
Eq.

3.19
, there is a linear dependence on
N_{\text{e}}
. However, as shown in
Example

3.4
,
c_{\text{J}}
scales as
1/N_{\text{e}}
when the environment is discretized from a continuous spectral density. Thus the overall constant
\mathfrak{c}
is independent of
N_{\text{e}}
in such cases.

Let us first prove the growth estimate for
t=t_{1}=\cdots=t_{n}=0
. We will address the time dependence later.

Proof of
Eq.

3.18
for
t=t_{1}=\cdots=t_{n}=0
.

Our goal is to show that
\|\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\sqrt{n!}\mathfrak{c}^{n}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any choice of
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}
. Since
\bm{E}_{\alpha}
’s are linear combinations of
\bm{b}_{k}
,
\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}
,
\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
, namely
\bm{E}_{\alpha}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\sum_{s=1}^{4}c^{s}_{\alpha,k}\bm{b}_{k,s}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}(\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0))\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\sum_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}=1}^{4}|p_{\alpha_{1},(k_{1},s_{1})}|\cdots|p_{\alpha_{n},(k_{n},s_{n})}|\|\bm{b}_{k_{1},s_{1}}\cdots\bm{b}_{k_{n},s_{n}}(\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0))\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}\sum_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}C_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{int}}\cdots C_{k_{n}}^{\mathrm{int}}\max_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}}\|\bm{b}_{k_{1},s_{1}}\cdots\bm{b}_{k_{n},s_{n}}(\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0))\|_{\text{tr}}

Here we have used that
\sum_{s=1}^{4}|p_{\alpha,(k,s)}|\leq 4^{\frac{1}{2}}(\sum_{s^{\prime}=1}^{4}|p_{\alpha,(k,s^{\prime})}|^{2})^{1/2}\leq 2(\sum_{s^{\prime}=1}^{4}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{N}|p_{\alpha^{\prime},(k,s^{\prime})}|^{2})^{1/2}=2C_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}
; where the first inequality holds by Cauchy-Schwarz. Now let us bound each term in the above sum. For any fixed
k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}
, let
n_{k}
be the number of times that index
k
appears in the list
k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}
. Then
\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}n_{k}=n
. Using the multi-mode estimate in
Corollary

3.8
, we have

\displaystyle C_{k_{1}}^{\mathrm{int}}\cdots C_{k_{n}}^{\mathrm{int}}\max_{s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}}\|\bm{b}_{k_{1},s_{1}}\cdots\bm{b}_{k_{n},s_{n}}(\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0))\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}(C_{k}^{\mathrm{int}})^{n_{k}}\sqrt{n_{k}!}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k})^{n_{k}}

Using that
C_{k}^{\mathrm{int}}\leq\sqrt{2c_{\text{J}}\omega_{k}}
from the infrared regularity condition, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}(C_{k})^{n_{k}}\sqrt{n_{k}!}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k})^{n_{k}}\leq\prod_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\sqrt{n_{k}!}(\sqrt{2c_{\text{J}}\omega_{k}}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k}))^{n_{k}}.

Using
\sqrt{\omega_{k}}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k})\leq\max\{\sqrt{\omega_{\text{M}}},\beta^{-1/2}\}=:C_{\beta,\omega_{\text{M}}}
, where
\omega_{\text{M}}=\max_{k}\omega_{k}
, we have

\prod_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\sqrt{n_{k}!}(\sqrt{2c_{\text{J}}\omega_{k}}c_{\beta}(\omega_{k}))^{n_{k}}\leq\prod_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\sqrt{n_{k}!}(\sqrt{2c_{\text{J}}}C_{\beta,\omega_{M}})^{n_{k}}\leq\sqrt{n!}(\sqrt{2c_{\text{J}}}C_{\beta,\omega_{M}})^{n}.

Combining all the above estimates, we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}(\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0))\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n!}\left(2\sqrt{2}N_{\text{e}}c_{\text{J}}C_{\beta,\omega_{M}}\right)^{n}.

∎

Next we prove the estimate for general
t\geq t_{1}\geq t_{2}\cdots\geq t_{n}\geq 0
. To address the time dependence in
Eq.

3.18
, it suffices to calculate the partial derivatives with respect to
t_{1}
,
\cdots
,
t_{n}
respectively. This involves the commutators between
\bm{E}_{\alpha_{i}}
and
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
, which we derived in Lemma
C.2
and Lemma
C.3
in
Appendix

C
.

Proof of time dependence in
Eq.

3.18
.

Note that the partial derivative of
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})
with respect to
t_{i}
(for
i=1,\cdots,n
), is a linear combination in the following form:

\sum_{\beta_{i}}c_{\beta_{i}}\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\beta_{i},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})

This is because if taking derivative with respect to
t_{i}
, we have
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t-t_{1})}\bm{E}_{\alpha_{1}}e^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}(t_{1}-t_{2})}\cdots\bm{E}_{\alpha_{n}}e^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t_{n}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)
with
\bm{E}_{\alpha_{i}}
replaced by
[\bm{E}_{\alpha_{i}},\bm{L}_{\text{e}}]
. This is because
\bm{E}_{\alpha}
is linear in
\{\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\}
,
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
is quadratic, and
\{\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\}
satisfies bosonic commutation relations, the commutator
[\bm{E}_{\alpha_{i}},\bm{L}_{\text{e}}]
is also the linear combination of
\{\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\}
(see
Eq.

C.2
,
Eq.

C.3
).
Then using
Eq.

C.2
,
Eq.

C.3
, the dynamics in
t_{i}
is controlled by a system of ODEs with the following matrix:

K=pK_{0}p^{+},\quad K_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}H-\Gamma&0&0&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}H^{\text{T}}-\Gamma^{\text{T}}&0&0\\
0&-2\Gamma^{\text{T}}&\mathrm{i}H^{\text{T}}+\Gamma^{\text{T}}&0\\
-2\Gamma&0&0&-\mathrm{i}H+\Gamma\end{pmatrix}

Here
K_{0}
is of size
4N_{\text{e}}\times 4N_{\text{e}}
,
K=pK_{0}p^{+}
is of size
N\times N
, and
p
is the
N\times 4N_{\text{e}}
coefficient matrix satisfying
\bm{E}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha,(k,s)}\bm{b}_{k,s}
, and
p^{+}
is its Moore-Penrose pseudo-inverse. Note that nonzero eigenvalues of
K=pK_{0}p^{+}
coincides with those of
K_{0}
. In the unitary case,
\Gamma=0
, thus all eigenvalues of
K
are purely imaginary. Then
\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\|_{\text{tr}}
is constant in all
t_{i}
’s, and the estimate in
Eq.

3.18
holds for all
t\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{n}\geq 0
with the same constant
\mathfrak{c}
.

For the dissipative case, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n})\|_{\text{tr}}\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{Kt_{n}}\|\cdot\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n-1},0)\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{Kt_{n-1}}\|\|\mathrm{e}^{Kt_{n}}\|\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,t_{1},\cdots,t_{n-2},0,0)\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\cdots\leq\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}(t,0,\cdots,0)\|_{\text{tr}}\mathrm{e}^{\gamma_{\max}(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{n})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}\right\|_{\text{tr}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\|\mathrm{e}^{Kt_{i}}\|\leq\left\|\mathcal{E}_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n}}\right\|_{\text{tr}}\left(\max_{s\in[0,t]}\|\mathrm{e}^{Ks}\|\right)^{n}.

Here
\|\cdot\|
denotes the matrix operator norm. In the last line we have used that
\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{e}}t}
is trace-preserving. Thus it suffices to multiply
\mathfrak{c}
by
\max_{s\in[0,t]}\|\mathrm{e}^{Ks}\|
.

We remark that in the case that
K
is diagonalizable,
\|\mathrm{e}^{Kt}\|
is bounded by
\mathrm{e}^{\gamma_{\max}t}
, where
\gamma_{\max}
is the maximum eigenvalue of
\Gamma
.
∎

3.1.4
Dynamics and error bound

We have introduced the environment Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}
and system-environment coupling
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}
.
Now, let us introduce the system Liouvillian.
The system Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}^{\text{U}}
from the system Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{s}}
is given by

\bm{L}_{\text{s}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{s}},\hat{\rho}].

The system Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\text{s}}
could be taken as any Hermitian operator acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{s}}
.
One typical physical example would be

\hat{H}_{\text{s}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}-\frac{\Delta_{j}}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{x}^{(j)}+\frac{h_{j}}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)},

where

\Delta_{j},h_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
are a tunneling constant and an energy gap between the two states of the
j
-th spin. One could also consider non-unitary system dynamics with the system Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}=\bm{L}_{\text{s}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{s}}
, where the system dissipator
\bm{D}_{\text{s}}
is defined as
\bm{D}_{\text{s}}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{q=1}^{n_{q}}\hat{l}_{q}\hat{\rho}\hat{l}_{q}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{l}_{q}^{\dagger}\hat{l}_{q},\hat{\rho}\}
for some
n_{q}
and
\hat{l}_{q}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{s}})
.

As a corollary of
Eq.

2.25
(see also
Eq.

2.28
), the dynamics of unitary and Lindblad spin-boson model are given by:

Corollary 3.10

(
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
for spin-boson model)
.

We have the following results for
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{+}\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\bm{F}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0),

(3.20)

\displaystyle\bm{F}(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\left(-c_{ij}(t_{1}-t_{2})\bm{\sigma}_{z}^{(i)}(t_{1})\bm{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}(t_{2})+c_{ij}(t_{1}-t_{2})\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{z}^{(i)}(t_{1})\bm{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}(t_{2})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-(c_{ij}(t_{1}-t_{2}))^{*}{\bm{\sigma}}_{z}^{(i)}(t_{1})\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{z}^{(j)}(t_{2})+(c_{ij}(t_{1}-t_{2}))^{*}\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{z}^{(i)}(t_{1})\widetilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{z}^{(j)}(t_{2})\right).

Here,
Eq.

3.20
is understood as a shorthand notation for an infinite series summation, as defined in
Eq.

2.30
.

c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
(
i,j=1,\cdots,n
) refers to the conventional bath correlation function (BCF) of the spin-boson model
[
BP07
, Sec. 3.2.4]
. For
t\geq t^{\prime}
, the coefficients
c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
(
i,j=1,\cdots,n
) are given as follows.

1.

For the unitary dynamics (
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
(see
Eq.

3.3
),
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
(see
Eq.

3.7
), with the initial environment density operator as the Gibbs state
Eq.

3.4
), we have

c(t-t^{\prime})=gG^{>}(t-t^{\prime})g^{\dagger}+(gG^{<}(t-t^{\prime})g^{\dagger})^{*},\quad t\geq t^{\prime}.

(3.21)

Note that here,
*
means complex conjugate (not Hermitian conjugate), and we introduce the greater and lesser Green’s functions
G^{>}(t-t^{\prime})
and
G^{<}(t-t^{\prime})
:

G^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H(t-t^{\prime})}\left(I-\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\right)^{-1},\quad G^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H(t-t^{\prime})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\beta H}-I\right)^{-1}.

(3.22)

2.

For the Lindblad dynamics (
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
(see
Eq.

3.3
),
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
(see
Eq.

3.7
), with the initial vacuum environment
Eq.

3.5
), we have

c(t-t^{\prime})=g\mathrm{e}^{(-\mathrm{i}H-\Gamma)(t-t^{\prime})}g^{\dagger},\quad t\geq t^{\prime}.

(3.23)

Here,
g
is an
n\times N_{\text{e}}
coefficient matrix in
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
,
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
, and
H,\Gamma
are
N_{\text{e}}\times N_{\text{e}}
Hermitian matrices in
Eq.

3.3
.

Since
\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
satisfy the CCR, and both

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
and
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
are quadratic in
\bm{b}_{k},\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}
, the Isserlis-Wick’s condition holds.
To apply the result of
Eq.

2.25
to get
Corollary

3.10
, we only need to verify that the superoperator BCF
C_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})
(
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1,\cdots,2n
) takes the following form:

Lemma 3.11

(BCFs for spin-boson model)
.

For
t\geq t^{\prime}
, the two-point superoperator correlation function
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(t-t^{\prime})
(
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1,\cdots,2n
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2i-1,2j-1}(t-t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime}),\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2i,2j}(t-t^{\prime})=-(c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime}))^{*},

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2i-1,2j}(t-t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})^{*},\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2i,2j-1}(t-t^{\prime})=c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime}),

where
c(t-t^{\prime})
is a
N_{\text{e}}\times N_{\text{e}}
matrix-valued function, given by
Eq.

3.21
,
Eq.

3.23

in the unitary and Lindblad dynamics, respectively.

Remark 3.2
.

The definitions of
G^{>}(t)
and
G^{<}(t)
in
Eq.

3.22
align with the standard convention for greater and lesser Green’s functions in the physics literature, differing only by a constant factor of
-\mathrm{i}
(for example, see
[
SVL13
, Section 6.1.3]
), namely,

G_{kl}^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=\operatorname{tr}\left(\hat{b}_{k}(t)\hat{b}_{l}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\right),\quad G_{kl}^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=\operatorname{tr}\left(\hat{b}_{l}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{b}_{k}(t)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)\right).

(3.25)

Here we have used the notation that for operator
\hat{O}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\text{e}}
,
\hat{O}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\hat{H}_{\text{e}}t}\hat{O}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\hat{H}_{\text{e}}t}
.
As a result,
c_{ij}(t,t^{\prime})
coincides with the bath correlation functions of the environment operators, namely,

c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})=\text{tr}(\hat{E}_{i}(t)\hat{E}_{j}(t^{\prime})\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)),\quad\hat{E}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}g_{ik}\hat{b}_{k}+g_{ik}^{*}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}.

(3.26)

Though in
Corollary

3.10
and
Lemma

3.11
we have only used
c(t-t^{\prime})
for
t\geq t^{\prime}
, using
Eqs.

3.26
and
3.25
, one can see that in unitary systems
Eqs.

3.21
and
3.22
still holds for
t<t^{\prime}
. In particular,
c(t-t^{\prime})
satisfies the following Hermitian property:

c(t-t^{\prime})=(c(t^{\prime}-t))^{\dagger}.

Remark 3.3
.

In the case of diagonal
\hat{H}_{\text{e}}
, i.e.,
H_{kl}=\omega_{k}\delta_{kl}
(
k,l=1,\cdots N_{\text{e}}
), and real coupling coefficient
g
, the BCF
c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
reduces to the widely-used form in literature (for example, see
[
BP07
, Eqs. (3.388) and (3.389)]
):

\displaystyle c_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}g_{ik}g_{jk}\left(\coth\left(\frac{\beta\omega_{k}}{2}\right)\cos(\omega_{k}(t-t^{\prime}))-\mathrm{i}\sin(\omega_{k}(t-t^{\prime}))\right).

(3.27)

Our results are applicable to the more general case, known as
coupled modes

[
MSS+20
,
LGVF24
,
HPCL25
]
, where
H
is a non-diagonal matrix.

We will prove
Lemma

3.11
in
Appendix

C
.
Furthermore, we have already shown that the estimate
Eq.

2.16
holds for this example in
Section

3.1.3
.
Then, as an application of
Theorem

2.9
and
Theorem

1.2
,
we have the following error bound for spin-boson models:

Corollary 3.12

(Error bound for spin-boson models)
.

Let
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
be the reduced system density operators of a unitary or Lindblad spin-boson model. Let
c_{ij}(t)
and
c_{ij}^{\prime}(t)
be the
correlation function corresponding to
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
, as defined by
Eq.

3.21
or
Eq.

3.23
. Then
the same error bounds as in
Theorem

2.9
for reduced system densities and
Theorem

1.2
for any bounded system observables
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
hold, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon t^{2}/2}-1,\quad|O_{\text{S}}(t)-O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)|\leq(\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon t^{2}/2}-1)\|\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\|,\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

(3.28)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon_{1}t}-1,\quad|O_{\text{S}}(t)-O_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)|\leq(\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon_{1}t}-1)\|\hat{O}_{\text{S}}\|,\quad\forall t\in[0,T],

where
\epsilon
and
\epsilon_{1}
are given by:

\epsilon=4n\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])},\quad\epsilon_{1}=4n\|c-c^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}.

We can directly apply
Corollary

3.12
to quantify the error bound of approximations in the pseudomode theory for the spin-boson model by considering the dynamics of
\hat{\rho}_{S}(t)
as the original unitary dynamics and the dynamics of
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{S}(t)
as the pseudomode dynamics from the Lindblad or quasi-Lindblad dynamics.

3.2
Fermionic impurity model

3.2.1
Fermionic environment

The fermionic environment Hilbert space is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}=\operatorname{span}\left(\left\{(\hat{c}_{1}^{\dagger})^{j_{1}}\cdots(\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger})^{j_{N_{\text{e}}}}|0\rangle_{\text{E}},j_{1},\cdots,j_{N_{\text{e}}}=0,1\right\}\right).

(3.29)

N_{\text{e}}
is the number of fermionic modes,
|0\rangle_{\text{E}}
is the environment vacuum state, and

\hat{c}_{k},\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
are the fermionic annihilation and creation operators for the
k
-th environment modes (
k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}
), which satisfy the canonical anti-commutation relations (CAR)
\{\hat{c}_{k},\hat{c}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\hat{1}_{\text{e}}
. We might want to define the following superoperators, just as in the bosonic case

Eq.

3.1
:

\bm{c}_{k}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{c}_{k}(\cdot),\quad\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}(\cdot),\quad\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}:(\cdot)\rightarrow(\cdot)\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\quad\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow(\cdot)\hat{c}_{k}.

However, in this definition, though
\bm{c}_{k}
and
\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
satisfies that
\{\bm{c}_{k},\bm{c}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\bm{1}_{\text{e}}
,

\bm{c}_{k}
and
\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}
actually commute rather than anticommute.
Thus, this definition does not satisfy the CAR, which is necessary for the Isserlis-Wick’s conditions (see
Definition

2.6
) to hold.
To overcome this, we introduce the parity operator
\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}})
defined as follows:

\hat{P}_{\text{e}}=(-1)^{\hat{N}_{\text{e}}},\quad\hat{N}_{\text{e}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{k}.

(3.30)

A useful property is that
\hat{P}_{\text{e}}
and
\hat{c}_{k},\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
anticommute, i.e.,
\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k}=-\hat{c}_{k}\hat{P}_{\text{e}}
,
\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}=-\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\text{e}}
.
With the parity operator, the fermionic environment superoperators are defined as follows:

Definition 3.13

(Fermionic environment superoperators)
.

For environment fermionic creation and annihilation operators
\hat{c}_{k}
,
\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
, let us define the following superoperators, denoted as

\bm{c}_{k}
,
\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
,
\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}
,
\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger}
(
k=1,\cdots,{N_{\text{e}}}
):

\displaystyle\bm{c}_{k}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k}(\cdot),\quad\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow-\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}(\cdot),\quad\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{P}_{\text{e}}(\cdot)\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\quad\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{P}_{\text{e}}(\cdot)\hat{c}_{k}.

(3.31)

Such definitions satisfy the CAR, summarized as follows,

Lemma 3.14

(Canonical anti-commutation relations for fermionic superoperators)
.

The bath superoperators
\bm{c}_{k},\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger}
(
k=1,\cdots,{N_{\text{e}}}
) satisfy the following canonical anti-commutation relations:

\displaystyle\left\{\bm{c}_{k},\bm{c}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right\}

\displaystyle=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\bm{1}_{\text{e}},\quad\left\{\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right\}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\bm{1}_{\text{e}},\quad

(3.32)

\displaystyle\left\{\bm{c}_{k},{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}\right\}=\left\{\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger},{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right\}=\left\{\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}\right\}=\left\{\bm{c}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}\right\}=\left\{\bm{c}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right\}=\left\{\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\right\}=0.

The environment Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
is given by

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{e}},\cdot]
,
\hat{H}_{\text{e}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}H_{kl}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{l}
,
where
H
is a
N_{\text{e}}\times N_{\text{e}}
Hermitian matrix.
Since
\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\bm{c}_{l}\hat{\rho}=-\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{l}\hat{\rho}=\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{l}\hat{\rho}=\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{l}\hat{\rho}
and

\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}\hat{\rho}=\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l}^{\dagger}(\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger})=\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{l}=\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{l}
,
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
could also be written in the following superoperator form:

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}=\sum_{k,l=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}-\mathrm{i}H_{kl}\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\bm{c}_{l}+\mathrm{i}H_{kl}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},

(3.33)

For the non-unitary dynamics, we focus on the specific setup used in
[
CAG19
,
PHZ+24
]
. Therein, the environment is divided into two separate parts: one with an initial vacuum environment and the other with an initial fully occupied environment, i.e.,

\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=(|\underbrace{0\cdots 0}_{N_{\text{1}}}\rangle\langle\underbrace{0\cdots 0}_{N_{\text{1}}}|)\otimes(|\underbrace{1\cdots 1}_{N_{\text{e}}-N_{1}}\rangle\langle\underbrace{1\cdots 1}_{N_{\text{e}}-N_{1}}|),

(3.34)

and there are no Hamiltonian and dissipative terms between the two different parts.
As a result, the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
is block-diagonal, i.e.,
H=\operatorname{diag}\{H_{1},H_{2}\}
, where
H_{1},H_{2}
are Hermitian matrices of size
N_{1}\times N_{1}
and
(N_{\text{e}}-N_{1})\times(N_{\text{e}}-N_{1})
.
The environment dissipator
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
is set such that the initial density operator becomes stationary,
\bm{D}_{\mathrm{e}}\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)=0
, thus according to
Eq.

2.39
, the two-point correlation functions
C_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t,t^{\prime})
depend only on
(t-t^{\prime})
. In particular, the dissipator has the following operator form,
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}=\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{-}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{+}
,

\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{-}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{N_{1}}2\Gamma^{-}_{kk^{\prime}}\left(\hat{c}_{k^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{k^{\prime}},\hat{\rho}\}\right),\quad\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{+}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}=N_{1}+1}^{N_{\text{e}}}2\Gamma^{+}_{ll^{\prime}}\left(\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{l^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{c}_{l^{\prime}}\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger},\hat{\rho}\}\right),

(3.35)

where
\Gamma^{-}
and
\Gamma^{+}
are positive semi-definite matrices with sizes
N_{1}\times N_{1}
and
(N_{\text{e}}-N_{1})\times(N_{\text{e}}-N_{1})
, respectively. In the superoperator form, using that
\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}\bm{c}_{k^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}=\hat{P}_{\text{e}}(\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k^{\prime}}\hat{\rho})\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}=\hat{c}_{k^{\prime}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
and
\bm{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}=-\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}(\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{l^{\prime}})=\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{l^{\prime}}
, we can see that
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{-}
and
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{+}
take the following superoperator form:

\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{-}=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{N_{1}}2\Gamma_{kk^{\prime}}^{-}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}\bm{c}_{k^{\prime}}-\Gamma_{kk^{\prime}}^{-}\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\bm{c}_{k^{\prime}}-\Gamma_{kk^{\prime}}^{-}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\text{ }\bm{D}_{\text{e}}^{+}=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}=N_{1}+1}^{N_{\text{e}}}2\Gamma_{ll^{\prime}}^{+}\bm{c}_{l}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l^{\prime}}^{\dagger}-\Gamma_{ll^{\prime}}^{+}\bm{c}_{l^{\prime}}\bm{c}_{l}^{\dagger}-\Gamma_{ll^{\prime}}^{+}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{l^{\prime}}^{\dagger}.

(3.36)

3.2.2
System-environment coupling and the fermionic superselection rule

The system Hilbert space is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}=\operatorname{span}\left(\left\{(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})^{j_{1}}\cdots(\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger})^{j_{n}}|0\rangle_{\text{S}},j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}=0,1\right\}\right),

(3.37)

where
|0\rangle_{\text{S}}
is a system vacuum state.
\hat{a}_{i}
and
\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}
(
i=1,\cdots,n
) are system fermionic annihilation and creation operators of
n
fermionic modes that also satisfy the CAR:
\{\hat{a}_{i},\hat{a}_{i^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\}=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\hat{1}_{\text{s}}
, where
\hat{1}_{\text{s}}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{s}})
is the system identity operator.
For
\hat{a}_{j}
,
\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
(
j=1,\cdots,n
), let us define the following superoperators, denoted as

\bm{a}_{j}
,
\bm{a}_{j}^{\dagger}
,
\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}
,
\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}^{\dagger}
:

\bm{a}_{j}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{a}_{j}(\cdot),\quad\bm{a}_{j}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}(\cdot),\quad\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}:(\cdot)\rightarrow-\hat{P}_{\text{s}}(\cdot)\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\text{s}},\quad\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}^{\dagger}:(\cdot)\rightarrow\hat{P}_{\text{s}}(\cdot)\hat{a}_{j}\hat{P}_{\text{s}}.

(3.38)

We introduce the system parity operator
\hat{P}_{\text{s}}=(-1)^{\hat{N}_{\text{s}}}
, where
\hat{N}_{\text{s}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i}
. Similar to
\hat{P}_{\text{e}}
, which anticommutes with
\hat{c}_{k}
and
\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
,
\hat{P}_{\text{s}}
also anticommutes with
\hat{a}_{i}
and
\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}
. Later, we will also use the parity operator of the entire extended system
\hat{P}=(-1)^{\hat{N}}=(-1)^{\hat{I}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{N}_{\text{s}}+\hat{N}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{I}_{\text{s}}}=\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{P}_{\text{s}}
.
The system superoperators in
Eq.

3.38
satisfy the similar anti-commutation rules as in
Eq.

3.32
.

For
i=1,\cdots,n

and
k=1,\cdots,N_{\text{e}}
, let us define
\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}
, which are extensions of system operators
\hat{a}_{i},\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
and environment operators
\hat{c}_{k},\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
onto the entire Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
In fermionic systems, such an extension could not be trivially defined as
\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{I}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes\hat{I}_{\text{s}}
, since in this case
\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}}
commute while they should anticommute.
As shown in the
Appendix

A
, the definition of

\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\hat{c}_{j}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}
should follow from the

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded structure of the Fock space (for example, see
[
Ber13
, Chap 3]
):

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger},\quad\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{a}_{i},\quad\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes\hat{I}_{\text{s}},\quad\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{c}_{k}\otimes\hat{I}_{\text{s}}.

(3.39)

The anti-commutation relationship can directly be shown from this definition:

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=(\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger})(\hat{c}_{k}\otimes\hat{I}_{\text{s}})=(\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\hat{c}_{k})\otimes(\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{I}_{\text{s}})

(3.40)

\displaystyle=-(\hat{c}_{k}\hat{P}_{\text{e}})\otimes(\hat{I}_{\text{s}}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger})=-(\hat{c}_{k}\otimes\hat{I}_{\text{s}})(\hat{P}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger})=-\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}.

Based on the extended operators, the system-environment Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
is given by

\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}\hat{\rho}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{SE}},\hat{\rho}],\quad\hat{H}_{\text{SE}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}(\nu_{ik}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}}+\nu_{ik}^{*}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}).

(3.41)

\hat{H}_{\text{SE}}
is a Hermitian operator known as the system-environment Hamiltonian,
\nu
is a
n\times N_{\text{e}}
coefficient matrix that describes the system-environment coupling. Since this is a fermionic system, due to the fermionic superselection rule
[
WWW52
]

2
2

2

Though according to the superselection rule we only need to consider
\hat{\rho}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
. However, mathematically, one could treat
\rho^{(0)}\notin\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
by keeping track of the action of the superoperators in both even and odd parity sectors. We refer to
[
CKC+22
]
for details.
, we have the following additional constraint for the intial density operator
\hat{\rho}(0)
:

\displaystyle\text{Fermionic superselection rule: }

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(0)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}}),

(3.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})=\{\hat{\rho}\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})|[\hat{\rho},\hat{P}]=0\}.

In other words,
\hat{\rho}(0)
commutes with the parity operator
\hat{P}
. We refer to
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
as the physical subspace of
B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
.
Since
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
is an invariant subspace for every term in
\bm{L}_{\text{E}}
,
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
, and later in
\bm{L}_{\text{S}}
, it suffices to consider their restrictions to the subspace
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})\subset B_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
. This reduction significantly simplifies the subsequent calculations.
We will show in
Lemma

C.5
that the Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
in
Eq.

3.41
could be written in the following superoperator form:

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{4n}\bm{E}_{\alpha}^{\text{U}}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha},

(3.43)

\displaystyle\bm{S}_{4j-3}

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\quad\bm{S}_{4j-2}=\bm{a}_{j},\quad\bm{S}_{4j-1}=\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}^{\dagger},\quad\bm{S}_{4j}=\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}

(3.44)

\displaystyle\bm{E}_{4j-3}^{\text{U}}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{jk}\bm{c}_{k},\quad\bm{E}_{4j-2}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{jk}^{*}\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\quad\bm{E}_{4j-1}^{\text{U}}=\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{jk}^{*}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\quad\bm{E}_{4j}^{\text{U}}=\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{jk}\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger},

(3.45)

for
j=1,\cdots,n
.
Note that both sides of the equality
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}=\sum_{i=1}^{4n}\bm{E}^{\text{U}}_{i}\otimes\bm{S}_{i}
is to be understood in the sense that both sides act on the subspace
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{S}})
.
For future use, let us define
\nu^{-}
,
\nu^{+}
as sublocks of
\nu
:
\nu=[\nu^{-},\nu^{+}]
, where
\nu^{-}
are of size
n\times N_{1}
, and
\nu^{+}
are of size
n\times(N_{\text{e}}-N_{1})
.

3.2.3
Dynamics and error bound

For fermionic impurity models, let us consider the following system Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\text{s}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ii^{\prime}=1}^{n}h_{ii^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i^{\prime}}+\sum_{ii^{\prime}j^{\prime}j=1}^{n}V_{ii^{\prime}j^{\prime}j}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}.

(3.46)

Here

h
is a
n\times n
Hermitian matrix,
V
is a
n\times n\times n\times n
tensor for electron-electron interaction. The system Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}
could be taken as the Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}^{\text{U}}=-\mathrm{i}[\hat{H}_{\text{s}},\cdot]
. One could also take non-unitary system dynamics, i.e.,
\bm{L}_{\text{s}}=\bm{L}_{\text{s}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{s}}
, where
\bm{D}_{\text{s}}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{q=1}^{n_{q}}\hat{l}_{q}\hat{\rho}\hat{l}_{q}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{l}_{q}^{\dagger}\hat{l}_{q},\hat{\rho}\}
for some
n_{q}
and
\hat{l}_{q}\in B(\mathcal{H}_{\text{s}})
.

As a corollary of
Eq.

2.25
(see also
Eq.

2.28
), the reduced system dynamics of the fermionic impurity model (for unitary, Lindblad and quasi-Lindblad dynamics) are given by:

Corollary 3.15

(
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
for fermionic impurity model)
.

We have the following results for
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\bm{L}_{\text{s}}t}\mathbf{T}^{-}\left(\mathrm{exp}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\bm{F}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)\right)\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(0),

(3.47)

\displaystyle\bm{F}(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}-\Delta^{<}_{ij}(t_{1}-t_{2})\left(\bm{a}_{i}^{\dagger}(t_{1})+\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{i}(t_{1})\right)\bm{a}_{j}(t_{2})-\Delta^{>}_{ij}(t_{1}-t_{2})\left(\bm{a}_{i}^{\dagger}(t_{1})+\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{i}(t_{1})\right)\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}^{\dagger}(t_{2})

\displaystyle+(\Delta_{ij}^{<}(t_{1}-t_{2}))^{*}\left(\bm{a}_{i}(t_{1})-\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}(t_{1})\right)\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{j}(t_{2})+(\Delta_{ij}^{>}(t_{1}-t_{2}))^{*}\left(\widetilde{\bm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}(t_{1})-\bm{a}_{i}(t_{1})\right)\bm{a}_{j}^{\dagger}(t_{2}).

Eq.

3.47
is understood as a shorthand notation for an infinite series summation, as defined in
Eq.

2.30
.
We refer to
\Delta^{>}_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
,
\Delta^{<}_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
(
i,j=1,\cdots,n
) as the greater and lesser hybridization function. For
t\geq t^{\prime}
,
\Delta^{>}_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
and
\Delta^{<}_{ij}(t-t^{\prime})
(
i,j=1,\cdots,n
) is defined as:

1.

For the unitary dynamics (
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
(see
Eq.

3.33
),
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
(see
Eq.

3.43
), with the initial environment density operator as the Gibbs state
Eq.

3.4
), we have

\Delta^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=\nu G^{>}(t-t^{\prime})\nu^{\dagger},\quad\Delta^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=\nu G^{<}(t-t^{\prime})\nu^{\dagger}.

(3.48)

The lesser and greater Green’s functions
G^{<}(t)
and
G^{>}(t)
are defined as:

G^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=\left(\mathrm{e}^{\beta H}+I\right)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H(t-t^{\prime})},\quad G^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=\left(I+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta H}\right)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}H(t-t^{\prime})}.

(3.49)

2.

For the Lindblad dynamics (
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
(see
Eqs.

3.33
and
3.36
),
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
(see
Eq.

3.43
), with the initial environment density operator in
Eq.

3.34
), we have

\Delta^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=\nu^{-}\mathrm{e}^{(-\mathrm{i}H^{-}-\Gamma^{-})(t-t^{\prime})}(\nu^{-})^{\dagger},\quad\Delta^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=\nu^{+}\mathrm{e}^{(-\mathrm{i}H^{+}-\Gamma^{+})(t-t^{\prime})}(\nu^{+})^{\dagger}.

(3.50)

Since
\bm{c}_{k},\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger}
satisfy the CAR (see
Eq.

3.32
), and both

\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}
and
\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
are quadratic in
\bm{c}_{k},\bm{c}_{k}^{\dagger},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k},\widetilde{\bm{c}}_{k}^{\dagger}
, the Isserlis-Wick’s condition holds.
Thus, according to
Eq.

2.25
,
Corollary

3.10
holds as long as the following lemma for the two-point BCFs holds:

Lemma 3.16

(BCFs for fermionic impurity model)
.

For
t\geq t^{\prime}
, the two-point correlation function
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1,\cdots,4n\right)
is given by the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4i-3,4j-2}(t-t^{\prime})=\mathcal{C}_{4i,4j-2}(t-t^{\prime})=-\Delta_{ij}^{>}(t-t^{\prime}),

(3.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4i-3,4j-1}(t-t^{\prime})=\mathcal{C}_{4i,4j-1}(t-t^{\prime})=-\Delta_{ij}^{<}(t-t^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4i-2,4j}(t-t^{\prime})=-\mathcal{C}_{4i-1,4j}(t-t^{\prime})=(\Delta_{ij}^{>}(t-t^{\prime}))^{*},

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4i-1,4j-3}(t-t^{\prime})=-\mathcal{C}_{4i-2,4j-3}(t-t^{\prime})=(\Delta_{ij}^{<}(t-t^{\prime}))^{*},

where
\Delta^{<}(t)
and
\Delta^{>}(t)
are the lesser and greater hybridization functions, given by
Eq.

3.48
,
Eq.

3.50

in
Corollary

3.15
, for the unitary and Lindblad dynamics, respectively.

Remark 3.4
.

Similar to
Remark

3.2
and
Eq.

3.27
,
the greater and lesser Green’s functions in
Eq.

3.49
follow the convention in condensed matter physics, differing only by a constant factor of
\mp\mathrm{i}
, where
G^{>}(t)
and
G^{<}(t)
are defined as in
Eq.

3.25
, with
\hat{b}_{k},\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
replaced by
\hat{c}_{k},\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}
. As a result, for unitary dynamics,
\Delta^{>}
and
\Delta^{<}
in
Eq.

3.48
coincides with the following operator BCFs:

\Delta_{ij}^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=\text{tr}(\hat{E}_{i}(t)\hat{E}_{j}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)),\quad\Delta_{ij}^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=\text{tr}(\hat{E}_{j}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{E}_{i}(t)\hat{\rho}_{\text{E}}(0)),\quad\hat{E}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{ik}\hat{c}_{k}.

Thus, also similar to the spin-boson case, though in
Corollary

3.15
and
Lemma

3.16
we have only used
\Delta^{>,<}(t-t^{\prime})
for
t>t^{\prime}
, using the above definition,
\Delta^{>,<}(t-t^{\prime})
could be extended to
t<t^{\prime}
. In other words, for unitary dynamics,
Lemma

3.16
still holds for
t<t^{\prime}
, and the Hermiticity property also holds, i.e.,

\Delta^{>}(t-t^{\prime})=(\Delta^{>}(t^{\prime}-t))^{\dagger},\quad\Delta^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=(\Delta^{<}(t^{\prime}-t))^{\dagger}.

If
\hat{H}_{\text{e}}
is diagonal, i,e,
H_{kl}=\omega_{k}\delta_{kl}
, then
\Delta^{\lessgtr}(t)
reduces to the following form, which is commonly used in non-equilibrium dynamical mean-field theory
[
ATE+14
]
:

\Delta_{ij}^{>}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{ik}\nu_{jk}^{*}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}t}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\omega_{k}}},\quad\Delta_{ij}^{<}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\text{e}}}\nu_{ik}\nu_{jk}^{*}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega_{k}t}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{\beta\omega_{k}}}.

(3.52)

We will prove
Lemma

3.16
in
Appendix

C
. Since here the fermionic
\mathcal{H}_{\text{E}}
is finite-dimensional,
Eq.

2.16
naturally holds, then as an application of
Theorem

2.9
and
Theorem

1.2
, we have the following error bound for fermionic impurity models:

Corollary 3.17

(Error bounds for fermionic impurity models)
.

Let
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
be the reduced system density operators of a unitary or Lindblad fermionic impurity model. Let
\Delta^{\lessgtr}_{ij}(t)
and
{\Delta^{\lessgtr}_{ij}}^{\prime}(t)
be the
lesser and greater hybridization functions corresponding to
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
, as defined by
Eq.

3.48
or
Eq.

3.50
. Then,
the same error bounds as in
Theorem

2.9
for reduced system densities and
Theorem

1.2
for any bounded system observables
\hat{O}_{\text{S}}
hold, i.e.,
Eq.

3.28
holds, in which

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle=4n\left(\|\Delta^{>}-{\Delta^{>}}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}+\|\Delta^{<}-{\Delta^{<}}^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\right),

(3.53)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}

\displaystyle=4n\left(\|\Delta^{>}-{\Delta^{>}}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}+\|\Delta^{<}-{\Delta^{<}}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}\right).

4
Applications to quasi-Lindblad dynamics

In this section, we discuss the applications of
Eq.

2.25
and
Theorem

2.9
to quasi-Lindblad dynamics.
For concreteness, we will focus on the quasi-Lindblad pseudomode introduced in
[
PHZ+24
]
, which preserves the Hermiticity of density operators without the CP condition. Our analysis is also relevant in other pseudomode formulations involving non-Hermitian dynamics
[
LACN19
,
PGP20
,
CLL+23
,
MFC+24
]
, and also for the hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM) method interpreted as quasi-Lindblad dynamics
[
XVK+23
,
ILL24
]
.

All quasi-Lindblad dynamics to date may suffer from subtle instability issues
[
WRTM17
,
DTR19
,
YXS20
,
LYS22
,
KS23
,
PHZ+24
]
. In particular, the dynamics can be asymptotically stable but non-contractive
[
PHZ+24
]
. Therefore, the differences between the two quasi-Lindblad dynamics could not be compared directly.
Nonetheless, by examining the proof of
Theorem

2.9
carefully, we find that it is sufficient for one of the dynamics to be contractive. In other words, the error analysis is still applicable when comparing a unitary or Lindblad dynamics, governed by
\bm{L}
, and a quasi-Lindblad dynamics, governed by
\bm{L}^{\prime}
. On the other hand, when comparing two quasi-Lindblad dynamics, we need to introduce an intermediary contractive dynamics denoted by
\tilde{\bm{L}}
, and then compare the distance between the dynamics generated by
\bm{L},\tilde{\bm{L}}
, and that by
\bm{L}^{\prime},\tilde{\bm{L}}
, respectively.

4.1
System-environment coupling

Apart from the system-environment coupling Liouvillian
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}
(
Eq.

3.7
and
Eq.

3.43
), the quasi-Lindblad pseudomode theory
[
PHZ+24
]
introduces an additional system-environment coupling Liouvillian term, denoted as
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
,
which takes a Lindblad-like dissipator form. In the spin-boson model,
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
is given by

\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{M}_{j}\hat{\rho}\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}|_{\mathcal{H}}+\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\rho}\hat{M}^{\dagger}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{\sigma_{z}}^{(j)}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{M}_{j}+\hat{M}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{\sigma_{z}}^{(j)}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{\rho}\},\quad\hat{M}_{j}=\sum_{k}2M_{jk}\hat{b}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}},

(4.1)

where
M
is a
n\times N_{\mathrm{e}}
complex coefficient matrix,
\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}|_{\mathcal{H}}
and
\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}
are the extensions of
\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}
and
\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}
to
\mathcal{H}
, defined as

\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{1}_{\text{e}}\otimes\hat{\sigma}_{z}^{(j)},\quad\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}=\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\otimes\hat{1}_{\text{s}}.

This new term
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
breaks the completely positive (CP) property. The superoperator form of
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
is given by

\displaystyle\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2n}\bm{E}_{\alpha}^{\text{D}}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\bm{E}_{2j-1}^{\text{D}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{e}}}\left(2M_{jk}^{*}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}-M_{jk}\bm{b}_{k}-M_{jk}^{*}\bm{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\right),\text{ }\bm{E}_{2j}^{\text{D}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{e}}}\left(2M_{jk}\bm{b}_{k}-M_{jk}^{*}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}-M_{jk}\widetilde{\bm{b}}_{k}^{\dagger}\right).\text{ }j=1,\cdots,n.

In the fermionic case, the system-environment Liouvillian
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
includes the coupling of the system with both parts of the environment, i.e.,
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{-}+\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{+}
, where
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{\mp}
has the following Lindblad-like structure:

\displaystyle\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{-}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{1}}M_{ik}^{*}(2\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}-\{\hat{c}_{k}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{\rho}\})+M_{ik}(2\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\rho}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}-\{\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{c}_{k}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{\rho}\}),

(4.3)

\displaystyle\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{+}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=N_{1}+1}^{N_{e}}M_{il}(2\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\rho}\hat{c}_{l}|_{\mathcal{H}}-\{\hat{c}_{l}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{\rho}\})+M_{il}^{*}(2\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{\rho}\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}-\{\hat{a}_{i}|_{\mathcal{H}}\hat{c}_{l}^{\dagger}|_{\mathcal{H}},\hat{\rho}\}),

where
M
is a
n\times N_{e}
coefficient matrix. Like in
Eq.

3.43
,
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{\mp}
could also be written in superoperator forms,

\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{\mp}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{4n}\bm{E}_{\alpha}^{\text{D}\mp}\otimes\bm{S}_{\alpha},

(4.4)

where both sides of
Eq.

4.4
is also understood to be superoperators acting on
\bm{L}^{(0)}(\mathcal{H})
(see
Eq.

3.42
). We refer to
Eq.

C.8
for expressions of environment superoperators
\bm{E}_{\alpha}^{\text{D}\mp}
(
\alpha=1,\cdots,4n
), and
Lemma

C.5
for proving that the operator and superoperator forms of
\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}^{\mp}
are equivalent. For future convenience, let us define
M^{-}
,
M^{+}
as sublocks of
M
:
M=[M^{-},M^{+}]
, where
M^{-}
are of size
n\times N_{1}
, and
M^{+}
are of size
n\times(N_{\text{e}}-N_{1})
.

4.2
Reduced system dynamics and error bound

For the reduced system density operator of quasi-Lindblad dynamics, we have the following result for reduced system density operator, also as the corollary of
Eq.

2.25
:

Corollary 4.1

(reduced system density operator of quasi-Lindblad dynamics)
.

We have the following result for quasi-Lindblad dynamics:

•

In the spin-boson case, where
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
(see
Eq.

3.3
),
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
(see
Eq.

3.7
,
Eq.

4.2
), and with the initial vacuum environment
Eq.

3.5
, we have that
Eq.

3.20
holds, where
c(t-t^{\prime})
is given by

c(t-t^{\prime})=(g-\mathrm{i}M)\mathrm{e}^{(-\mathrm{i}H-\Gamma)(t-t^{\prime})}\left(g^{\dagger}-\mathrm{i}M^{\dagger}\right),\quad t\geq t^{\prime}.

(4.5)

•

In the fermionic impurity case, where
\bm{L}_{\text{e}}=\bm{L}_{\text{e}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{e}}
(see
Eqs.

3.33
and
3.36
),
\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}=\bm{L}_{\text{SE}}^{\text{U}}+\bm{D}_{\text{SE}}
(see
Eqs.

3.43
and
4.4
),with the initial environment density given by
Eq.

3.34
), we have that
Eq.

3.47
holds, where
\Delta^{>}(t)
and
\Delta^{<}(t)
are given by

\displaystyle\Delta^{>}(t)

\displaystyle=(\nu^{-}-\mathrm{i}M^{-})\mathrm{e}^{(-\mathrm{i}H^{-}-\Gamma^{-})t}((\nu^{-})^{\dagger}-\mathrm{i}(M^{-})^{\dagger}),

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Delta^{<}(t)

\displaystyle=(\nu^{+}-\mathrm{i}M^{+})\mathrm{e}^{(-\mathrm{i}H^{+}-\Gamma^{+})t}((\nu^{+})^{\dagger}-\mathrm{i}(M^{+})^{\dagger}).

Similar to
Corollary

3.10
and
Corollary

3.15
, according to
Eq.

2.17
,
Corollary

4.1
holds as long as the following lemma for superoperator BCFs hold:

Lemma 4.2

(BCFs for quasi-Lindblad dynamics)
.

For BCFs of quasi-Lindblad dynamics, we have

•

For spin-boson model,
Eq.

3.24
holds, where
c(t-t^{\prime})
is given by
Eq.

4.5
;

•

For fermionic impurity model,
Eq.

3.51
holds, where
\Delta^{<}
,
\Delta^{>}
are given by
Eq.

4.6
.

We will prove
Lemma

4.2

in
Appendix

C
.
As we have emphasized above,
since the quasi-Lindblad dynamics break the contraction property, one can not compare two quasi-Lindblad dynamics directly. However, by introducing an intermediate contractive dynamics, we have the following result:

Corollary 4.3

(Error bound for quasi-Lindblad dynamics)
.

Let
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t),\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
be the reduced system density operator of two quasi-Lindblad dynamics. In the case of spin-boson model, let
c_{ij}(t)
and
c_{ij}^{\prime}(t)
be the
correlation function corresponding to
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
, as defined by
Eq.

4.5
, and let
\widetilde{c}(t)

be the BCFs of an arbitrary contractive (unitary or Lindblad) dynamics (
Eq.

3.21
or
Eq.

3.23
). Then
Eq.

3.28
holds,
where
\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}
is defined as:

\epsilon=4n(\|c-\widetilde{c}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}+\|c^{\prime}-\widetilde{c}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}),\quad\epsilon_{1}=4n(\|c-\widetilde{c}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}+\|c^{\prime}-\widetilde{c}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}).

Simiarly, in the fermionic impurity case, let
\Delta^{\lessgtr}_{ij}(t)
and
{\Delta^{\lessgtr}_{ij}}^{\prime}(t)
be the
lesser and greater hybridization functions corresponding to
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
,
\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)
, as defined by

Eq.

4.6
, and let
\widetilde{\Delta}^{\lessgtr}_{ij}(t)
be the hybridization functions corresponding to any unitary or Lindblad dynamics (
Eq.

3.48
or
Eq.

3.50
). Then
Eq.

3.28
holds, where
\epsilon,\epsilon_{1}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle=4n\left(\|\Delta^{>}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}+\|\Delta^{<}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}+\|{\Delta^{>}}^{\prime}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}+\|{\Delta^{<}}^{\prime}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}\right),

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}

\displaystyle=4n\left(\|\Delta^{>}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}+\|\Delta^{<}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}+\|{\Delta^{>}}^{\prime}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{>}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}+\|{\Delta^{<}}^{\prime}-{\widetilde{\Delta}^{<}}\|_{L^{1}([0,T])}\right).

Proof.

Let us prove the case of spin-boson models while the case of fermionic impurity follows similarly.
Let
\widetilde{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)
be the reduced system density operator corresponding to
\widetilde{c}(t)
.
Since
Theorem

1.2
could be used for the contractive dynamics and the quasi-Lindblad dynamics, we have:

\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\widetilde{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\widetilde{\epsilon}t^{2}/2}-1,\quad\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}t^{2}/2}-1,\quad

where
\widetilde{\epsilon}=4n(\|c-\widetilde{c}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}
and
\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}=\|c^{\prime}-\widetilde{c}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}\leq\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)-\widetilde{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}+\|\hat{\rho}_{\text{S}}^{\prime}(t)-\widetilde{\rho}_{\text{S}}(t)\|_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\widetilde{\epsilon}t^{2}/2}-1+\mathrm{e}^{\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}t^{2}/2}-1=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\widetilde{\epsilon}t^{2}/2)^{n}+(\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}t^{2}/2)^{n}/n!\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}((\widetilde{\epsilon}+\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime})t^{2}/2)^{n}=\mathrm{e}^{{\epsilon}t^{2}/2}-1.

The error bound in the
L^{1}
sense holds in a similar way.
∎
